Massive Inflationary Amplitudes: Differential Equations and Complete Solutions for General Trees
Haoyuan Liu and Zhong-Zhi Xianyu

Abstract
We construct and solve a complete system of differential equations for general tree-level inflation correlators with an arbitrary number of massive scalar exchanges and time-dependent couplings. Any massive tree correlators can be uniquely fixed by solving this system of equations with appropriate boundary conditions. We take a hybrid approach to solve this system, using the differential equation to get the inhomogeneous solution and the bulk time integrals to determine the homogeneous solution. Altogether, we obtain analytical results for all tree-level massive inflation correlators with generic kinematics, expressed as multivariate hypergeometric series of energy ratios. The result can be neatly organized as a sum of the completely inhomogeneous solution, which we call the massive family tree, and all of its cuts. As simple applications, we provide full analytical expressions for tree correlators with one, two, and three massive exchanges.



USTC-ICTS/PCFT-24-56

1  Introduction

There have been considerable interests and efforts recently in the study of quantum field theoretic amplitudes in the cosmological background, including the wavefunction coefficients and the equal-time correlation functions. Theory-wise, these amplitudes possess fascinating structures that crystalize many properties of QFTs in cosmological backgrounds. Phenomenologically, they underlie the large-scale correlations of primordial curvature or tensor perturbations, and encode rich physical information about the primordial inflationary universe.

A major boost of this direction came from a related program of cosmological collider (CC) physics
[

,

,

,
36
,

,

,
39
,
40
,
41
,

,
43
,

,

,

,

,
51
,
52
]
, which aims at probing heavy new particles and their interactions at the inflation scale. In CC physics, the central observables are the oscillatory shapes in the scalar/tensor correlation functions generated by resonant interactions between heavy spectator fields and curvature/tensor perturbations. Thus, CC physics necessitates the study of correlators or wavefunction coefficients mediated by massive fields, which we collectively call massive inflationary amplitudes.

To compute massive correlators appearing in realistic CC models can be a very challenging task. The challenges come in several ways: First, propagators for massive fields in the cosmological background are necessarily special functions (typically Hankel functions or Whittaker functions in inflation) with time orderings, and thus we need to perform time-ordered integrals over products of these special functions. Second, many CC processes involve loop graphs and loop integrals can be nontrivial. Third, CC processes with large signals typically break some spacetime symmetries, including dS boosts (special conformal transformations) and scale invariance (dilatation).

Despite all these challenges, the study of massive inflation correlators has received great boosts in recent years. Several new techniques have been proposed and many results for massive correlators have been obtained. The program of cosmological bootstrap was proposed to compute massive inflationary correlators by solving differential equations
[
53
]
. The original works considered the case with full or weakly broken dS isometries and succeeded in bootstrapping tree graphs with a single massive exchange
[
54
,
55
]
. Later studies improved the methods and obtained results with strongly broken dS boosts and/or dilatation
[
56
,
57
,
58
,
59
]
. In some cases, it is possible to obtain closed-form expressions without infinite sums
[
60
]
. However, the differential-equation method quickly becomes nontrivial beyond the single massive exchange, as the order and number of differential equations increase fast with the number of massive lines. Currently, the state-of-the-art result from the differential equation approach is the double massive exchanges in a few degenerate configurations
[
61
]
. See also
[
62
,
63
,
64
]
for related works.

Incidentally, things get much simplified if one considers the special case of conformal scalar with mass
m^{2}=2H^{2}
(
H
being the Hubble parameter) instead of scalars of general masses, due to the enhanced symmetry
[
65
]
. In this case, a system of differential equations has been constructed for arbitrary tree graphs in power-law FRW universes. From the boundary viewpoint, this system emerges from a rather sophisticated kinematic flow
[
66
,
67
]
. However,
[
68
]
showed that the full analytical solutions to this system can be easily written down by computing the bulk time integrals directly using the family-tree decomposition (FTD). Later it was realized that the differential equations for the family tree integrals are also much simpler
[
69
]
. See also
[
70
,
71
,
72
,
73
,
74
]
for related works.

At the same time, there is an independent line of developments which focus directly on the bulk time integrals for the correlators
[
75
,
76
,
58
,
77
,
78
,
79
,
80
,
81
,
82
,
83
]
. Two useful techniques in this direction are the
partial Mellin-Barnes representation (PMB)
[
76
,
58
]
and the family tree decomposition (FTD)
[
80
,
68
]
. The PMB representation resolves all special functions in the bulk modes into powers, so that the bulk integrals become arbitrarily nested time integrals over powers and exponential functions
1
1

1

See
[
84
,
85
,
86
,
87
]
for a related full Mellin-space approach.
. The family-tree decomposition is then designed to compute such integrals with the results in a special class of multivariate hypergeometric functions, called family trees. Then, the analytical computation of any tree diagrams is reduced to a routine of collecting poles of the Mellin integrands, which can be trivially done for generic tree graphs. To the best of our knowledge, before this work, the combination of PMB and FTD is the only method that can compute tree correlators with an arbitrary number of massive exchanges. Meanwhile, the numerical computation of tree graphs has also been systematized recently
[
88
,
89
,
90
]
. Beyond the tree level, things are less explored, although progress has also been made at 1-loop and multi-loop orders, including full analytical or numerical results, as well as analytical results for CC signals
[
75
,
77
,
79
,
78
,
81
,
83
]
.

Given the methods of PMB and FTD, the problem of analytically computing any massive tree correlators has been solved. However, the situation is not fully satisfactory. First, results obtained with PMB and FTD are not in an optimal form, in the sense that their series representations typically involve too many layers of summations: For a tree diagram with
I
internal lines, this method yields a series with
3I
summation variables, among which
2I
layers come from PMB and
I
from FTD
[
80
]
. On the other hand, as we shall see, a diagram is fully specified by
2I
independent kinematic variables. So, if the final answer is to be expressed as a hypergeometric function of
2I
variables, we would reasonably conjecture that this function involves only
2I
layers of summations. To find these more economic expressions for general trees was one of the original motivations for this work.

Second, going through the procedure of PMB and FTD means that we still need to take Mellin representation for special functions, do the FTD, and then collect all relevant poles in the Mellin integrand. This is a trivial procedure, but still involves quite an amount of calculations when the graph becomes large. In this regard, we were further motivated by the analytical results for conformal scalar amplitudes in
[
68
]
. There, we have a very simple set of rules to write down the full analytical results for arbitrary conformal scalar tree amplitudes, in terms of a family tree integral and its cuts. We were led to suspect that similar rules exist for arbitrary masses, not only the conformal value
m^{2}=2H^{2}
. Were this true, we would be able to directly write down the full analytical results for arbitrary massive tree correlators without doing any real computations.

We achieve this goal in the current work, eliminating the need to do tedious computations, and providing full analytical results for an arbitrary number of massive exchanges for generic kinematics. (By generic kinematics, we mean that all
2I
kinematic variables are unconstrained by equalities and can vary independently. Constrained cases will be called degenerate.) In particular, we show that the concept of family trees can be properly generalized to arbitrary masses with a compact formula, and that a generic tree diagram can be expressed in terms of a massive family tree and its cuts. The result is in an optimal form, in the sense that the number of summations is reduced to
2I
, matching the number of independent variables. Since the whole procedure only requires us to take a finite number of products and summations of massive family trees, we could say that computing tree correlators in inflation has been made as simple as in flat space, where we take products and sums of propagators and couplings.

Summary of main results

The final answer, albeit simple, is obtained by going through a rather long journey, along which we also get a number of intermediate results, including a complete system of differential equations for arbitrary trees, recursion relations for building trees from smaller graphs, the cuts of trees, and the relations between the cuts and the CC signals. These results are useful and interesting in their own rights. Thus, before delving into the details in subsequent sections, we outline the basic strategy and summarize the main results of this paper.

After defining massive tree amplitudes and briefly reviewing the standard diagrammatic methods for their computations in Sec.
2
, we derive a complete set of differential equations for a general tree graph with an arbitrary number of massive exchanges, with couplings carrying arbitrary power-law time dependence in Sec.
3
. For a tree graph of
V
vertices and thus
I=V-1
internal lines, there are
2I
independent kinematic variables, and the system consists of
2I
coupled second-order partial differential equations, given in (
14
) and (
15
) and illustrated in Fig.
1
. We show that these equations can be conveniently derived with the standard method of inserting a Klein-Gordon operator and doing integration by parts. The final system is a coupled Lauricella system
[
91
]
.

Then, from Sec.
4
onward, we set out to solve the differential equations. The strategy is to build the completely inhomogeneous solution (CIS) first, and then obtain the homogeneous solutions as its cuts. Following this strategy, in Sec.
4
we derive two recursion formulae, (
61
) and (
77
), to generate inhomogeneous solutions for adding a new line to any massive tree graph. (See Fig.
4
.) Then, in Sec.
5
, we use the recursion formulae to build the CIS to an arbitrary massive tree graph. It is somewhat striking that the result can be expressed as a very simple formula (
100
), which can be thought of as a generalization of family trees to massive cases. Thus we call it a massive family tree. Then, in Sec.
6
, we construct the homogeneous solutions by cutting the CIS, and the rule for taking a cut is summarized in (
119
). (See also Fig.
10
.) The full answer for any tree diagram is then presented in Sec.
7
together with a few explicit examples, including tree diagrams with single, double, and triple exchanges. Further discussions and outlooks are left to Sec.
8
.

Notations and conventions

We work with
(3+1)
-dimensional dS spacetime with metric
{\mathrm{d}}s^{2}=(-{\mathrm{d}}\tau^{2}+{\mathrm{d}}\bm{x}^{2})/(H\tau)^{2}
with
\tau\in(-\infty,0)
the conformal time and
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
the comoving coordiates. The Hubble parameter
H
is set to unity,
H=1
, throughout the work. We draw heavy use of shorthand notations such as
E_{ij}=E_{i}+E_{j}
and
q_{1\cdots N}\equiv q_{1}+\cdots+q_{N}
. We use the diagrammatic notation of
[
92
]
for Feynman diagrams in Schwinger-Keldysh formalism. We also use the massive family tree notation
\big\llbracket{\cdots}\big\rrbracket
, similar to
[
68
]
, which is reviewed in App.
A.2
. We list some frequently used symbols in App.
A
and useful mathematical functions in App.
B
.

2  Brief Review of Massive Inflationary Amplitudes

In this section, we set the stage by briefly reviewing the perturbative construction of general massive amplitudes, and fixing our notations and conventions. As in
[
68
]
, we use inflationary amplitudes to mean two different types of objects: the wavefunction coefficients and the (equal-time) correlators. They are conceptually different objects but share a lot of technical similarities. Therefore, it is very convenient to consider the both at the same time. A slightly more detailed introduction to these objects can be found in
[
68
]
. More comprehensive reviews on this subject can be found in
[
92
]
for correlation functions and
[
93
]
for wavefunction coefficients. Below we only list the essential equations to be self-contained.

Massive correlators

In cosmology, the most relevant correlators are those of a massless scalar or tensor field during inflation. Also, in more theoretical studies, one often considers a conformal scalar field (with mass
m^{2}=2
) since it has a simple mode function closely related to a massless scalar mode. Below, we will use the conformal scalar as an example for illustration, and the generalization to the massless scalar is trivial.

We assume that the conformal scalar
\phi_{c}
and a bunch of massive scalar fields
\sigma_{\alpha}
are directly and weakly coupled in the bulk via polynomial interactions. (Derivative couplings or spinning exchanges introduce additional tensor structures that can be factored out and are irrelevant to our current study.) The scalar
\sigma_{\alpha}
has mass
m^{2}_{\alpha}>9/4
so that the corresponding
mass parameter

\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}\equiv\sqrt{m_{\alpha}^{2}-9/4}
is real. The assumption of
m^{2}_{\alpha}>9/4
is only for easier presentation; Generalization to arbitrary mass is trivial as long as their couplings are IR-finite. Also, we will simply call
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}
the mass in the following when no confusion could arise.

Then, we consider a general tree-graph contribution to the
N
-point correlator of
\phi_{c}
, where all internal lines are from exchanges of massive scalars
\sigma_{\alpha}
. (The case of exchanging an arbitrary number of conformal scalars is much easier and has been completely solved in
[
68
]
). We can conveniently derive the diagrammatic expansion and the corresponding Feynman rules in Schwinger-Keldysh (SK) formalism. See
[
92
]
for a pedagogical introduction. As a result, we can directly compute a tree graph as a bulk time integral over products of couplings and propagators:

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu(\bm{k}_{1},\cdots,\bm{k}_{N})=\sum_{\mathsf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathsf{a}_{V}=\pm}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\prod_{i=1}^{V}\Big[{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{i}\,\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}(-\tau_{i})^{P_{i}}\Big]\prod_{n=1}^{N}C_{\mathsf{a}_{n}}(k_{n},\tau_{n})\prod_{\alpha=1}^{I}D_{\mathsf{a}_{\alpha}\mathsf{a}_{\alpha}^{\prime}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}(K_{\alpha};\tau_{\alpha},\tau_{\alpha}^{\prime}),

(1)

where we are considering a tree graph with
N
external lines,
I
internal lines, and
V
vertices. Clearly, for a tree diagram, we have
V=I+1
. It is understood that the time and SK indices in the propagators should be properly identified with the corresponding variables of the vertices to which they attach. Below let us explain this expression more explicitly.

According to the diagrammatic rule, we assign a time integral
\int{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{i}
to each interaction vertex, together with a factor of
+\mathrm{i}
(a consequence of real and direct couplings), an SK index
\mathsf{a}_{i}=\pm
due to the closed path integral, and a monomial factor
(-\tau_{i})^{P_{i}}
accounting for the background evolution and possible time-dependent couplings. We omit trivial multiplicative coupling constants at all vertices throughout this work.

Then, for each external line, we assign a bulk-to-boundary propagator
C_{\mathsf{a}_{n}}(k_{n},\tau_{n})
of a conformal scalar field, whose explicit form is:

C_{\mathsf{a}}(k;\tau)=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\tau\tau_{f}}\,}{2k}e^{\mathsf{a}\mathrm{i}k\tau},

(2)

where
\tau_{f}<0
is a late-time cutoff introduced to take care of the late-time scaling behavior of a conformal scalar, and has no real significance for our calculation below.
For each internal line, on the other hand, we assign a bulk massive propagator
D_{\mathsf{a}_{\alpha}\mathsf{a}_{\alpha}^{\prime}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}(K_{\alpha};\tau_{\alpha},\tau_{\alpha}^{\prime})
with arbitrary mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}>0
. There are four types of bulk propagators. The two with opposite SK indices
D_{\mp\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}
(Wightman functions) are factorized and solve the homogeneous Klein-Gordon equation for the massive scalar in dS, and the two with the same SK indices
D_{\pm\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}
(Feynman and anti-Feynman propagators) are nested in time and solve the Klein-Gordon equation with a
\delta
-function source. Explicitly, they can be expressed in terms of Hankel function
\text{H}^{(i)}_{\nu}(z)

(i=1,2)
as:

\displaystyle D_{-+}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(K;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{4}e^{-\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{3/2}\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(-K\tau_{1})\mathrm{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(-K\tau_{2}),

(3)

\displaystyle D_{+-}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(K;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{4}e^{-\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{3/2}\mathrm{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(-K\tau_{1})\mathrm{H}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(-K\tau_{2}),

(4)

\displaystyle D_{\pm\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(K;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=

\displaystyle~D_{\mp\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(K;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})+D_{\pm\mp}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(K;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1}),

(5)

It turns out convenient to introduce a dimensionless version of the graph, which is related to the original graph
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu(\bm{k}_{1},\cdots,\bm{k}_{N})
in (
1
) by trivial rescaling. The rescaling is based on two useful properties of propagators. The first property is about the bulk-to-boundary propagators (
2
): Suppose there are
A
external lines attached to the vertex
\tau_{i}
. Then,

\displaystyle\prod_{n=1}^{A}C_{\mathsf{a}_{i}}(k_{n};\tau_{i})(-\tau_{i})^{P_{i}}=\bigg(\prod_{n=1}^{A}\displaystyle\frac{\,{-\tau_{f}}\,}{2k_{n}}\bigg)e^{+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}E_{i}\tau_{i}}(-\tau_{i})^{P_{i}+A},

(6)

where
E_{i}\equiv k_{1}+\cdots+k_{A}
will be called the
vertex energy
in the following. Of course, the parameter
P_{i}
on each vertex gets shifted after using the explicit expressions for external lines. We denote the shifted exponent by
p_{i}\equiv P_{i}+A
, and call it the
twist
of the vertex. Thus, we see that, if we remove the trivial factors
-\tau_{f}/(2k_{n})
, the graph
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu{\mathcal{G}}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
will depend only on
V
vertex energies
\{E_{1},\cdots,E_{V}\}
, together with the energy on all internal lines
\{K_{1},\cdots,K_{I}\}
, which we call
line energies
.

The second property is about the bulk propagators (
3
)-(
5
):

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(K_{\alpha}\tau_{i},K_{\alpha}\tau_{j})\equiv K_{\alpha}^{3}D_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(K_{\alpha};\tau_{i},\tau_{j}).

(7)

That is, the dimensionless bulk propagator
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}
depends on the line energy and the two time variables only through the two dimensionless combinations
K_{\alpha}\tau_{i}
and
K_{\alpha}\tau_{j}
.

The above two properties motivate us to define the
dimensionless graph

{\mathcal{G}}(\{E\},\{K\})
in the following way:

\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu\mathcal{G}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu(\bm{k}_{1},\cdots,\bm{k}_{N})=\bigg(\prod_{n=1}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\,{-\tau_{f}}\,}{2k_{n}}\bigg)\bigg(\prod_{i=1}^{V}\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{E_{i}^{1+p_{i}}}\bigg)\bigg(\prod_{\alpha=1}^{I}\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{K_{\alpha}^{3}}\bigg)\mathcal{G}(\{E\},\{K\}).

(8)

The notation
\mathcal{G}(\{E\},\{K\})
highlights that the dimensionless graph
{\mathcal{G}}
is fully determined by the
I
line energies and
V=I+1
vertex energies. However, since
{\mathcal{G}}
is dimensionless, it depends on these
2I+1
variables only through their ratios. Therefore, we have the freedom to remove one more variable so that there are only
2I
independent energy ratios
2
2

2

In (
8
), all energy variables are assumed nonvanishing. When an energy variable is set to zero, we should slightly modify the definition of a dimensionless graph. We consider an example with a zero vertex energy in Sec.
7.4
.
.

A convenient choice for the
2I
independent energy ratios can be obtained as follows. For any internal line with energy
K_{\alpha}
with two endpoints with vertex energies
E_{i}
and
E_{j}
, we can form two energy ratios:

\displaystyle r_{(\alpha i)}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{E_{i}},

\displaystyle r_{(\alpha j)}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{E_{j}}.

(9)

Then, for a total of
I
internal lines, we can form
2I
energy ratios
\{r_{A}\}

A=1,\cdots,2I
. It is clear that these
2I
energy ratios are independent variables in a generic graph. However, there are special cases in which some of these ratios are constrained. For instance, in the case of a two-point mixing between an external line and an internal line with line energy
K
, the vertex energy of the two-point vertex is also
K
, resulting in an energy ratio constrained to be 1. We call situations like this degenerate kinematics. In this work, we only consider nondegenerate kinematics, where all
2I
energy ratios can vary independently. We call such cases generic.

It is easy to see that, in the generic case, the
2I
energy ratios
\{r_{A}\}
are not only independent but also complete, in the sense that they fully specify the kinematics of a dimensionless graph
{\mathcal{G}}
. To make this point clear, we introduce the dimensionless time
z_{i}
at each vertex:

z_{i}\equiv E_{i}\tau_{i}.

(10)

Then, it is straightforward to see that the dimensionless graph
{\mathcal{G}}
can be computed from the following integral:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}(\{E\},\{K\})=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathsf{a}_{V}=\pm}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\prod_{i=1}^{V}\Big[{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}}\Big]\prod_{\alpha=1}^{I}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{a}_{j}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}(r_{(\alpha i)}z_{i},r_{(\alpha j)}z_{j}).

(11)

Again, it is understood that the
i,j
indices appearing in the rescaled bulk propagator
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
should be identified with the labels of
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
’s two endpoints.

To summarize, for a general tree-graphic contribution
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu{\mathcal{G}}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
to a massive inflation correlator, we can construct a dimensionless graph
{\mathcal{G}}
, which is related to
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu{\mathcal{G}}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
by trivial rescaling, and is fully specified by a vertex energy
E_{i}
and a twist
p_{i}
at each vertex, together with a line energy
K_{\alpha}
and a mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}
for each internal line. Below, we will work exclusively with the dimensionless graph
{\mathcal{G}}
, and we will simply call it a graph for short. No confusion could arise since the original graph
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu{\mathcal{G}}\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
is always hatted.

Wavefunction coefficients

The wavefunction coefficients of a theory are defined by expanding the Schrödinger-picture wavefunctional around the Gaussian ansatz. These coefficients have diagrammatic expansions quite similar to correlators, and are also computed by bulk time integrals. Explicitly, for the same diagram as specified above, the corresponding wavefunction coefficient
{\Psi}
is computed by:

\displaystyle{\Psi}(\bm{k}_{1},\cdots,\bm{k}_{N})=\int_{-\infty}^{0}\prod_{i=1}^{V}\Big[{\mathrm{d}}\tau_{i}(+\mathrm{i})(-\tau_{i})^{P_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}E_{i}\tau_{i}}\Big]\prod_{\alpha=1}^{I}G^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(K_{\alpha};\tau_{\alpha},\tau_{\alpha}^{\prime}).

(12)

For wavefunction coefficients, the bulk-to-boundary propagator of a conformal scalar is simply
B(k;\tau)=e^{\mathrm{i}k\tau}
. So, unlike correlators, we don’t have to amputate the wavefunction coefficients by removing factors
-\tau_{f}/(2k_{n})
. It is automatically a function of vertex energies
E_{i}
and line energies
K_{\alpha}
. The bulk propagator
G^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}
for a scalar of mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
, is given by:

\displaystyle G^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(K;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{4}e^{-\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{3/2}\bigg[\text{H}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(-K\tau_{1})\text{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(-K\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})

\displaystyle+\text{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(-K\tau_{1})\text{H}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(-K\tau_{2})\theta(\tau_{2}-\tau_{1})-\displaystyle\frac{\,{\text{H}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(1)}(-K\tau_{f})}\,}{\text{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(-K\tau_{f})}\text{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(-K\tau_{1})\text{H}_{-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}^{(2)}(-K\tau_{2})\bigg].

(13)

Importantly, the bulk propagator satisfies the same Klein-Gordon equation with
\delta
-function source as the Feynman propagator
D_{++}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}
. They are distinguished by the boundary conditions: The Feynman propagator is specified by the Bunch-Davies initial condition with canonical normalization, while the bulk propagator for the wavefunction coefficients is determined by the vanishing boundary condition at the future boundary
G^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(K;\tau_{1}\rightarrow 0,\tau_{2})=G^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(K;\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\rightarrow 0)=0
. As a result, the system of differential equations satisfied by a tree graph of a wavefunction coefficient is identical to the one for the correlator of the same graph, although the solutions are different in two cases due to different boundary conditions. Therefore, from now on, we will focus on correlators, and the wavefunction coefficients can be treated in a similar fashion.

3  Differential Equations for Massive Tree Correlators

In this section, we first present the system of differential equations for arbitrary tree-level massive inflation correlators, and then provide the details of the derivation. From now on, we only focus on correlators, and it will become clear that the same set of equations also applies to wavefunction coefficients.

3.1
The differential equations

In this subsection, we present the differential equations satisfied by an arbitrary tree graph
{\mathcal{G}}
in (
11
). As a preparation, we first introduce the concept of graph contraction.

Figure 1:
Graphic representation of the differential equation (
14
) for the graph
{\mathcal{G}}
with respect to Line
\alpha
and Vertex
i
.

For an arbitrary graph
{\mathcal{G}}
, we define its
contraction
with respect to its internal line with label
\alpha
to be a new dimensionless graph, denoted as
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}[{\mathcal{G}}]
. Let the two endpoints of Line
\alpha
be Vertices
i
and
j
, with vertex energies
E_{i}
,
E_{j}
, and twists
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
, respectively. Then, the contracted graph
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}[{\mathcal{G}}]
is obtained by the following two steps:

1.

Remove Line
\alpha
in
{\mathcal{G}}
and pinch Vertices
i
and
j
together as a new effective vertex;

2.

Assign the vertex energy
E_{ij}
and twist
p_{ij}+4
to the new effective vertex.

The operation of contraction is quite intuitive: When pinching the two vertices together, we are not changing the total energies flowing into these two vertices from
external
lines. So the vertex energy of the new vertex is simply the sum of the original two. On the other hand, in a twist parameter
p_{i}
, there is always a contribution of
-4
coming from the metric determinant
\sqrt{-g}=a^{4}(\tau)=\tau^{-4}
. When pinching the two vertices into one, we need to remove one metric determinant and collect the remaining time powers, and this is why we have
p_{ij}+4
for the new vertex, instead of
p_{ij}
.

With the above preparation, we can now present the system of differential equations for
{\mathcal{G}}
. There are
2I
independent equations, where
I
is the number of internal lines. (See (
11
).) Each of these
2I
equations can be specified as follows. First, we pick up an internal line, say Line
\alpha
(with line energy
K_{\alpha}
and mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}
), which connects Vertex
i
(with vertex energy
E_{i}
and twist
p_{i}
) and Vertex
j
(with
E_{j}
and
p_{j}
). Second, we pick up one vertex (say Vertex
i
) from the two endpoints of Line
\alpha
. Then, there is an energy ratio
r_{(\alpha i)}=K_{\alpha}/E_{i}
as introduced before, and there is a corresponding differential operator
\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha i)}
that contracts the graph
{\mathcal{G}}
to
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}[{\mathcal{G}}]
. Explicitly, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha i)}{\mathcal{G}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{(\alpha i)}^{p_{j}+4}r_{(\alpha j)}^{p_{i}+4}}\,}{\big[r_{(\alpha i)}+r_{(\alpha j)}\big]^{p_{ij}+5}}\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}[{\mathcal{G}}],

(14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha i)}\equiv\Big(\vartheta_{(\alpha i)}-\displaystyle\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}\Big)^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}^{2}-r_{(\alpha i)}^{2}\big(\vartheta_{\{i\}}+p_{i}+2\big)\big(\vartheta_{\{i\}}+p_{i}+1\big).

(15)

where
p_{ij}\equiv p_{i}+p_{j}
,
\vartheta_{(\alpha i)}\equiv r_{(\alpha i)}(\partial/\partial{r_{(\alpha i)}})
is the Euler operator associated with the energy ratio
r_{(\alpha i)}
, and
\vartheta_{\{i\}}
is the sum of Euler operators for all line energies attached to Vertex
i
:

\vartheta_{\{i\}}\equiv\sum_{\beta\in\mathcal{N}(i)}\vartheta_{(\beta i)}.

(16)

Here we use
\mathcal{N}(i)
to denote the neighbor of Vertex
i
, namely, the set of all lines attached to
i
.

The
2I
differential equations in (
14
) form a complete set of differential equations and can fully determine the graph with appropriate boundary conditions, which we will show in subsequent sections. Incidentally, it is straightforward to show that all
2I
operators
\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha i)}
defined in (
15
) commute with each other:

\displaystyle\big[\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha i)},\mathcal{D}_{(\beta j)}\big]=0.

(17)

Example: 3-site chain

Let us spell out all equations with a concrete example. Consider a 3-site chain as shown in Fig.
2
, which is a tree graph with 3 vertices, 2 bulk massive lines with masses
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}
and
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}
, and an arbitrary number of external lines. The dimensionless graph is fully specified by 3 twists
p_{i}
, 2 masses
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}

(\alpha=1,2)
, and 4 energy ratios
r_{1}\equiv K_{1}/E_{1}
,
r_{2}\equiv K_{1}/E_{2}
,
r_{3}\equiv K_{2}/E_{2}
, and
r_{4}\equiv K_{2}/E_{3}
. We assume that all 4 energy ratios are independent variables. Then, a complete set of differential equations can be written as:

\displaystyle\Big[\big(\vartheta_{r_{1}}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}\big)^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}^{2}-r_{1}^{2}(\vartheta_{r_{1}}+p_{1}+2)(\vartheta_{r_{1}}+p_{1}+1)\Big]{\mathcal{G}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}^{p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}}(r_{1},\cdots,r_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[\big(\vartheta_{r_{2}}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}\big)^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}^{2}-r_{2}^{2}(\vartheta_{r_{2}}+\vartheta_{r_{3}}+p_{2}+2)(\vartheta_{r_{2}}+\vartheta_{r_{3}}+p_{2}+1)\Big]{\mathcal{G}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}^{p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}}(r_{1},\cdots,r_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{1}^{p_{2}+4}r_{2}^{p_{1}+4}}\,}{r_{12}^{p_{12}+5}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}^{(p_{12}+4)p_{3}}(r_{1}r_{3}/r_{12},r_{4}),

(18)

\displaystyle\Big[\big(\vartheta_{r_{3}}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}\big)^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}^{2}-r_{3}^{2}(\vartheta_{r_{2}}+\vartheta_{r_{3}}+p_{2}+2)(\vartheta_{r_{2}}+\vartheta_{r_{3}}+p_{2}+1)\Big]{\mathcal{G}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}^{p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}}(r_{1},\cdots,r_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big[\big(\vartheta_{r_{4}}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}\big)^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}^{2}-r_{4}^{2}(\vartheta_{r_{4}}+p_{3}+2)(\vartheta_{r_{4}}+p_{3}+1)\Big]{\mathcal{G}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}^{p_{1}p_{2}p_{3}}(r_{1},\cdots,r_{4})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{3}^{p_{3}+4}r_{4}^{p_{2}+4}}\,}{r_{34}^{p_{23}+5}}{\mathcal{G}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}^{p_{1}(p_{23}+4)}(r_{1},r_{4}r_{2}/r_{34}).

(19)

These equations can be graphically expressed as in Fig.
2
.

Figure 2:
The system of differential equations for the 3-site chain graph.

3.2
Deriving the differential equations

Now we derive the system of differential equations (
14
) satisfied by the dimensionless graph
{\mathcal{G}}
in (
11
). The basic strategy is obvious: We insert a Klein-Gordon operator into the integrand of the graph, to annihilate one bulk propagator or to collapse it into a
\delta
function, so that the whole integral becomes simpler, being either zero or a smaller graph with one less vertex. This simpler graph will be the “right-hand side” of our differential equations. For the “left-hand side,” we use integration by parts to switch the order of the Klein-Gordon operator and the integral, so that we get a differential operator that directly acts on the whole graph. Equating both sides gives the desired equation. Below we spell out this procedure.

Dimensionless propagator

By definition, the bulk propagator of a massive scalar field is a solution to the Klein-Gordon equation with or without a localized source. In momentum space, this statement takes the following form:

\displaystyle\big(\tau_{1}^{2}\partial_{\tau_{1}}^{2}-2\tau_{1}\partial_{\tau_{1}}+K^{2}\tau_{1}^{2}+m^{2}\big)D_{\mathsf{a}_{1}\mathsf{a}_{2}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(K;\tau_{1},\tau_{2})=-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{1}(\tau_{1}\tau_{2})^{2}\delta(\tau_{1}-\tau_{2})\delta_{\mathsf{a}_{1}\mathsf{a}_{2}},

(20)

and there is another similar equation with all
\tau_{1}\rightarrow\tau_{2}
in the above Klein-Gordon operator. One can check this equation directly from the explicit expressions in (
3
)-(
5
).

Now, in terms of the dimensionless propagator
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{1}\mathsf{a}_{2}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{1}z_{1},r_{2}z_{2})
, the above Klein-Gordon equation can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\big[\vartheta_{z_{1}}^{2}-3\vartheta_{z_{1}}+(r_{1}^{2}z_{1}^{2}+m^{2})\big]\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{1}\mathsf{a}_{2}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{1}z_{1},r_{2}z_{2})=-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{1}(r_{1}z_{1}r_{2}z_{2})^{2}\delta(r_{1}z_{1}-r_{2}z_{2})\delta_{\mathsf{a}_{1}\mathsf{a}_{2}},

(21)

where
\vartheta_{z_{1}}\equiv z_{1}\partial_{z_{1}}
. This will be a key equation in our following derivation of the differential equations for the graph. Incidentally, it is worth noting that the dimensionless propagator
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{1}\mathsf{a}_{2}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{1}z_{1},r_{2}z_{2})
satisfies a trivial yet useful relation:

\displaystyle\vartheta_{z_{1}}^{n}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{1}\mathsf{a}_{2}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{1}z_{1},r_{2}z_{2})=

\displaystyle~\vartheta_{r_{1}}^{n}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{1}\mathsf{a}_{2}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{1}z_{1},r_{2}z_{2}),

(22)

with
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Note in particular that this identity does not depend on the choices of SK indices.

Differential Operator

To be specific, we consider an arbitrary internal line, say Line
\alpha
, which connects two vertices, called Vertices
i
and
j
, and we want to derive a differential equation associated with the ratio
r_{(\alpha i)}=K_{\alpha}/E_{i}
. It will be useful to further specify that there are
L\geq 1
internal lines attached to Vertex
i
(including Line
\alpha
), and we label them by
(\alpha,\beta_{2},\cdots,\beta_{L})
. The other ends of these
L
internal lines will be labeled by
(j,i_{2},\cdots,i_{L})
, respectively. Similarly, we assume that there are
M\geq 1
internal lines attached to Vertex
j
(including Line
\alpha
), and we label them by
(\alpha,\gamma_{2},\cdots,\gamma_{M})
. The other ends of these
M
internal lines will be labeled by
(i,j_{2},\cdots,j_{M})
, respectively. To avoid clutter, we use a temporary notation for energy ratios, defined as follows, instead of using
r_{(\alpha i)}
:

\displaystyle r_{i}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{E_{i}},

\displaystyle r_{j}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{E_{j}},

\displaystyle r_{n}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\beta_{n}}}\,}{E_{i}},

\displaystyle r_{n}^{\prime}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\beta_{n}}}\,}{E_{i_{n}}},

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{m}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\gamma_{m}}}\,}{E_{j}},

\displaystyle\bar{r}_{m}^{\prime}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\gamma_{m}}}\,}{E_{j_{m}}},

(23)

where
n=2,\cdots,L
and
m=2,\cdots,M
. We show our labeling of the graph in Fig.
3
.

Figure 3:
Labelling of graph
{\mathcal{G}}
for the derivation of its differential equations.

Now, in the integral (
11
), we can isolate the
z_{i}
-dependent part as follows:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{a}_{1},\cdots,\mathsf{a}_{V}=\pm}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\prod_{\ell\neq i}\Big[{\mathrm{d}}z_{\ell}\,\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{\ell}(-z_{\ell})^{p_{\ell}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{\ell}z_{\ell}}\Big]\prod_{\beta\notin\mathcal{N}(i)}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{\ell}\mathsf{a}_{n}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta})}(r_{\ell}z_{\ell},r_{n}z_{n})\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,\mathbf{A}_{i},

(24)

For brevity, we suppress all indices and arguments of
{\mathcal{G}}
here and below. In (
24
), we introduced
\mathbf{A}_{i}
as the integrand of the
z_{i}
-integral:

\mathbf{A}_{i}\equiv\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{a}_{j}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}\big(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j}\big)}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\prod_{n=2}^{L}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{a}_{i_{n}}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}})}\big(r_{n}z_{i},r_{n}^{\prime}z_{i_{n}}\big)}.

(25)

Here and following, we use the same color code as lines in Fig.
3
for the bulk propagators. To derive the differential equation of
{\mathcal{G}}
with respect to
r_{(\alpha i)}=r_{i}
, we consider the following expression:

\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,{\mathbf{D}}_{\alpha}\mathbf{A}_{i},

(26)

where
\mathbf{D}_{\alpha}\mathbf{A}_{i}
is the integrand of
z_{i}
with an additional Klein-Gordon operator acting on Line
\alpha
from the side of Vertex
i
:

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}_{\alpha}\mathbf{A}_{i}\equiv

\displaystyle~\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\prod_{n=2}^{L}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{a}_{i_{n}}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}})}\big(r_{n}z_{i},r_{n}^{\prime}z_{i_{n}}\big)}

\displaystyle\times\Big\{\Big[\vartheta_{z_{i}}^{2}-3\vartheta_{z_{i}}+\big(r_{i}^{2}z_{i}^{2}+m_{\alpha}^{2}\big)\Big]{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{a}_{j}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}\big(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j}\big)}\Big\}.

(27)

To get the left-hand side of the differential equation, we want to pull the Klein-Gordon operator in (
27
) out of the integral. This is a trivial task for
\vartheta_{z_{i}}^{2}-3\vartheta_{z_{i}}
thanks to the relations such as (
22
). The mass term
\propto m_{\alpha}^{2}
is trivial as well. Thus, the only trouble is from the term
\propto r_{i}^{2}z_{i}^{2}
. To deal with it, we use the following relation:

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,z_{i}^{2}\mathbf{A}_{i}=-\big(\vartheta_{\{i\}}+p_{i}+2\big)\big(\vartheta_{\{i\}}+p_{i}+1\big)\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,\mathbf{A}_{i},

(28)

where
\vartheta_{\{i\}}
is the Euler operator associated with all line energies attached to Vertex
i
, as defined in (
16
). This relation is easily derived using integration by parts, as shown in the box below.

Derivation of (
28
)
We abbreviate the propagators by:

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}\equiv\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{a}_{j}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}\big(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j}\big),

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}}\equiv\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{a}_{i_{n}}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}})}\big(r_{n}z_{i},r_{n}^{\prime}z_{i_{n}}\big).~~~~(n=2,\cdots,L)

(29)

Then, we begin with the following equality:

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,\partial_{z_{i}}\bigg[-(-z_{i})^{p_{i}+1}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}\prod_{n=2}^{L}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}}\Big[(p_{i}+1)+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}+z_{i}\partial_{z_{i}}\Big]\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}\prod_{n=2}^{L}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}}\bigg[(p_{i}+1)+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}+\vartheta_{r_{i}}+\sum_{n=2}^{L}\vartheta_{r_{n}}\bigg]\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}\prod_{n=2}^{L}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}}.

(30)

In the last line, we have used the relation (
22
). Also, the operator
\vartheta_{r_{i}}+\sum\limits_{n=2}^{L}\vartheta_{r_{n}}
is nothing but
\vartheta_{\{i\}}
as defined in (
16
). Thus we get:

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,z_{i}\mathbf{A}_{i}=-\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}}\big(\vartheta_{\{i\}}+p_{i}+1\big)\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,\mathbf{A}_{i}.

(31)

Likewise, we consider the following the identity:

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle-\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,\partial_{z_{i}}\bigg[(-z_{i})^{p_{i}+2}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}\prod_{n=2}^{L}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,(-z_{i})^{p_{i}+1}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}}\Big[(p_{i}+2)+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}+\vartheta_{\{i\}}\Big]\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}\prod_{n=2}^{L}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}}.

(32)

So we get:

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,z_{i}^{2}\mathbf{A}_{i}=-\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}}\big(\vartheta_{\{i\}}+p_{i}+2\big)\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,z_{i}\mathbf{A}_{i}.

(33)

Combining (
31
) and (
33
), we get (
28
).

Then, using (
22
) and (
28
), we can rewrite the expression in (
26
) as:

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,{\mathbf{D}}_{\alpha}\mathbf{A}_{i}=

\displaystyle~\Big[\vartheta_{r_{i}}^{2}-3\vartheta_{r_{i}}-r_{i}^{2}\big(\vartheta_{\{i\}}+p_{i}+2\big)\big(\vartheta_{\{i\}}+p_{i}+1\big)+m_{\alpha}^{2}\Big]\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,\mathbf{A}_{i}.

(34)

Here
\vartheta_{r_{i}}\equiv r_{i}\partial_{r_{i}}
. Obviously, all these differential operators trivially penetrate all other integrals and summations in (
24
), so that the left-hand side of our differential equation will be:

\Big[\Big(\vartheta_{(\alpha i)}-\displaystyle\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}\Big)^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}^{2}-r_{(\alpha i)}^{2}\big(\vartheta_{\{i\}}+p_{i}+2\big)\big(\vartheta_{\{i\}}+p_{i}+1\big)\Big]{\mathcal{G}},

(35)

where we have restored the previous notation
\vartheta_{r_{i}}=\vartheta_{(\alpha i)}
and
r_{i}=r_{(\alpha i)}
, completed the square of
\vartheta_{(\alpha i)}
, and used the relation
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}^{2}=m_{\alpha}^{2}-9/4
.

Source term

For the right-hand side, we use Klein-Gordon equation (
21
) to evaluate (
26
), finish the sum over SK index
\mathsf{a}_{i}
, and get:

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{a}_{i}=\pm}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,{\mathbf{D}}_{\alpha}\mathbf{A}_{i}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{a}_{i}=\pm}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}}{\,\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\prod_{n=2}^{L}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{a}_{i_{n}}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}})}\big(r_{n}z_{i},r_{n}^{\prime}z_{i_{n}}\big)}

\displaystyle\times(-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i})\big[r_{i}z_{i}r_{j}z_{j}\big]^{2}\delta\big(r_{i}z_{i}-r_{j}z_{j}\big)\delta_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{a}_{j}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}^{p_{i}+4}}\,}{r_{i}^{p_{i}+1}}(-z_{j})^{p_{i}+4}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{j}(r_{j}/r_{i})z_{j}}{\,\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\prod_{n=2}^{L}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{j}\mathsf{a}_{i_{n}}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}})}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}r_{n}}\,}{r_{i}}z_{j},r_{n}^{\prime}z_{i_{n}}\Big)}.

(36)

To see what it gives us, let us now consider the
z_{j}
-integral in the original graph (
24
):

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{j}(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{j})(-z_{j})^{p_{j}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{j}z_{j}}{\,\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\prod_{\ell=2}^{M}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{j}\mathsf{a}_{j_{\ell}}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\gamma_{\ell}})}\big(\bar{r}_{\ell}z_{j},\bar{r}_{\ell}^{\prime}z_{j_{\ell}}\big)}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}^{p_{i}+4}}\,}{r_{i}^{p_{i}+1}}(-z_{j})^{p_{i}+4}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{j}(r_{j}/r_{i})z_{j}}{\,\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\prod_{n=2}^{L}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{j}\mathsf{a}_{i_{n}}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}})}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}r_{n}}\,}{r_{i}}z_{j},r_{n}^{\prime}z_{i_{n}}\Big)}.

(37)

As shown here, the
z_{j}
-integrand includes two parts: In the first line, we have factors that come from the original integrand of the graph (
24
), and, in the second line, we have contributions from evaluating (
36
).

The
z_{j}
-integrand in (
37
) almost has the form of the integrand for a standard graph, except that the exponent in the exponential factor is not properly “normalized.” To normalize this exponent, we change the integral variable
z_{j}
to
\tilde{z}_{j}\equiv(1+r_{j}/r_{i})z_{j}
(and therefore
z_{j}=\tilde{z}_{j}\times r_{i}/r_{ij}
, where
r_{ij}=r_{i}+r_{j}
). Then, the integral in (
37
) becomes:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}^{p_{j}+4}r_{j}^{p_{i}+4}}\,}{r_{ij}^{p_{ij}+5}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tilde{z}_{j}\,(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{j})(-\tilde{z}_{j})^{p_{ij}+4}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{j}\tilde{z}_{j}}{\,\color[rgb]{0,0.75,0.16}\prod_{\ell=2}^{M}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{j}\mathsf{a}_{j_{\ell}}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\gamma_{\ell}})}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}\bar{r}_{\ell}}\,}{r_{ij}}\tilde{z}_{j},\bar{r}_{\ell}^{\prime}z_{j_{\ell}}\Big)}{\,\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\prod_{n=2}^{L}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{j}\mathsf{a}_{i_{n}}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta_{n}})}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}r_{n}}\,}{r_{ij}}\tilde{z}_{j},r_{n}^{\prime}z_{i_{n}}\Big)}.

(38)

Now, including all remaining time integrals and summations in (
24
), we get the final expression for the right-hand side of our differential equation. These all look rather complicated. Let us spell out their meanings in the following.

1.

After the
z_{i}
-integral in (
36
), Vertices
i
and
j
are effectively pinched together into one vertex (which is still called Vertex
j
). The new Vertex
j
now has its own time integral (
38
). Clearly, the new Vertex
j
connects
L+M-2
bulk lines.

2.

To see the meaning of the complicated
r
-factors in (
38
), we restore the energy variables. First, the coefficients for the first arguments of bulk propagators can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{r_{ij}}\times\bar{r}_{\ell}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{j}}\,}{E_{ij}}\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\gamma_{\ell}}}\,}{E_{j}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\gamma_{\ell}}}\,}{E_{ij}},

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}}\,}{r_{ij}}\times r_{n}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{ij}}\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\beta_{n}}}\,}{E_{i}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\beta_{n}}}\,}{E_{ij}}.

(39)

The meaning is clear: These factors amount to giving a vertex energy
E_{ij}=E_{i}+E_{j}
to the new Vertex
j
, replacing original vertex energies (
E_{j}
at Vertex
j
and
E_{i}
at Vertex
i
).

3.

The factor
(-\tilde{z}_{j})^{p_{ij}+4}
tells us that the twist of the new Vertex
j
is
p_{ij}+4
. As mentioned before, it simply means that we collect all time powers from the couplings at both ends of the pinched line, and include a term
+4
to remove one metric determinant
\sqrt{-g}=a^{4}=\tau^{-4}
.

4.

Finally, the prefactor in front of the integral in (
38
) can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}^{p_{j}+4}r_{j}^{p_{i}+4}}\,}{r_{ij}^{p_{ij}+5}}\equiv\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{E_{ij}}\Big)^{3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{ij}}\Big)^{p_{i}+1}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{j}}\,}{E_{ij}}\Big)^{p_{j}+1}.

(40)

It is now easy to recognize that this prefactor comes from our definition of the dimensionless graph (
8
).

Combining all these points, we see that the right-hand side of the equation is nothing but the factor (
40
) multiplied by
\mathsf{C}_{\alpha}[{\mathcal{G}}]
, namely, the contraction of the original graph
{\mathcal{G}}
with respect to Line
\alpha
. This completes the derivation of the differential equation with respect to
r_{(\alpha i)}
. Clearly, we can repeat this procedure for all
2I
independent energy ratios, which leads to
2I
independent differential equations in (
14
).

3.3
Strategy of solving the differential equation system

Before presenting our construction of solutions, let us briefly describe the general structure of the solution. These solutions are multivariate hypergeometric functions that are not very well understood. Therefore, our strategy is to find series expansions in parameter regions of interest. Clearly, one specific series expansion is convergent only in a finite region. It is thus important to know how to expand the solution in different regions.

It is beyond the scope of this work to develop expansions of these hypergeometric functions at all regions, which is likely a nontrivial mathematical problem. Instead, we will focus on regions that are particularly useful and telling for physical applications. Explicitly, we will consider regions where all line energies
K_{\alpha}
can become arbitrarily soft compared to (some of) vertex energies
E_{i}
, which we call the
signal reigion
.

It is known that
K_{\alpha}\rightarrow 0
limit for any given
\alpha
(with all other energies staying in the interior of the physically accessible region) is typically a branch point of the correlator, which we call the
signal branch point
. At this point, the correlator breaks into three pieces, one nonanalytic in
K_{\alpha}
, one analytic in
K_{\alpha}
but nonanalytic in some other vertex energies, and one analytic in all energies. Using the terminology of cosmological collider physics, they are respectively called the nonlocal signal, the local signal, and the background with respect to the
K_{\alpha}\rightarrow 0
limit.

From the viewpoint of bulk time integrals, the signals (both nonlocal and local) are from the parts of the
K_{\alpha}
-propagators that are independent of time-ordering
\theta
-functions, and are thus explicitly factorized. On the contrary, the background comes totally from the nested part of the
K_{\alpha}
-propagator. Technically, the signals are relatively easier to compute since they do not involve any nested time integral, and the backgrounds are hard.

From the viewpoint of differential equations derived above, the signals associated with the
K_{\alpha}
-propagator correspond to the pieces of the correlator that are annihilated by the two operators
\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha i)}
and
\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha j)}
(assuming
K_{\alpha}
line is attached to the two vertices
i
and
j
), and are thus the homogeneous solutions to the
\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha i)}
- and
\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha j)}
-equations. As often happens, the determination of these homogeneous solutions requires appropriate boundary conditions. On the other hand, the background corresponds to a piece that is not annihilated, but contracted by
\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha i)}
and
\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha j)}
. Thus, the background corresponds to the inhomogeneous solution to the
\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha i)}
- and
\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha j)}
-equations.

The above descriptions apply to any of the internal lines in a graph. Altogether, when we think of a massive tree graph in the region where all line energies are small, the whole graph exhibits a nice pattern, which can be schematically expressed as:

{\mathcal{G}}=\sum_{c_{1}=\,\text{S,B}}\cdots\sum_{c_{I}=\,\text{S,B}}{\mathcal{G}}[c_{1},\cdots,c_{I}].

(41)

Here, for each internal line
K_{\alpha}
(
\alpha=1,\cdots,I
), we introduce a parameter
c_{\alpha}
, which takes two possible values S and B, respectively corresponding to the signal/homogeneous and the background/inhomogeneous part with respect to
K_{\alpha}
. As mentioned above, every signal can be further decomposed into a nonlocal signal and a local signal. Therefore, a tree graph with
I
internal lines can be decomposed into
3^{I}
distinct pieces according to its analytical behavior around all signal branch points.

Therefore, to find the complete solution for a massive tree graph in the signal region, we only need to find explicit expressions for all terms
{\mathcal{G}}[c_{1},\cdots,c_{I}]
. As mentioned, the more lines taking
c_{\alpha}=\;
B, the harder to get the solution. The hardest part is thus the piece with all lines nested. We will call it the completely inhomogeneous solution (CIS). At the other extreme, the piece with all lines factorized is pretty straightforward. As we shall see below, they are simply given by products of Lauricella hypergeometric functions. Of course, there are many things in between.

Our strategy is to tackle the hardest part first, namely, to find the CIS of an arbitrary graph with all
c_{\alpha}=\;
B in (
41
). Then, we will show that, changing a
c_{\alpha}
from B to S simply amounts to taking an appropriate cut of CIS. As we will show, taking a single cut to CIS breaks the CIS into a product of two dressed CISs for two subgraphs. The multiple cuts can be executed in a similar way. Thus, all terms in (
41
) can be expressed as a sum of products of (dressed) CISs of the whole graph and its subgraphs. Therefore, as long as we know how to write down the CIS and how to do the cut (namely, how to dress the CIS of a subgraph), we can immediately get the full solution, in the following form:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}=\sum_{\text{cuts}}\text{CIS}\;[{\mathcal{G}}],

(42)

where we sum over
2^{I}
possible cuts of the CIS, including the zero cut. We will achieve this result step by step in the following three sections.

It will be useful to have an idea of the form of the final solution before we solve for them explicitly. Schematically, the series expansion of the CIS of a graph has the following form:

\displaystyle\text{CIS}\,\big[{\mathcal{G}}\big]=\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}f\big(\{\ell,m\}\big)\prod_{i=2}^{V}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{i}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{i}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+p_{i}+1}.

(43)

That is, we are expanding the CIS as a power series of
1/E_{1}
(
3
3

3

Like the original family tree integrals in
[
80
,
68
]
, expanding in a large vertex energy limit is only one of many possible expansion schemes for the CIS. It would be interesting to find more of them. We leave this topic for a future work.
). The series is going to converge if all energy ratios
K_{\alpha}/E_{1}
and
E_{i}/E_{1}
are small. Therefore, in practice, we need to pick up the largest vertex energy, call it
E_{1}
, and apply the above expansion. In this series, we have
2(V-1)=2I
summation variables
\ell_{i},m_{i}

(i=2,\cdots,V)
, respectively collecting powers of all
I
line energies
K_{i}
and
I
remaining vertex energies
E_{i}
. Notice that we have used the same subscript
i
to label the line and non-maximal vertex energies, because, after picking up the maximal energy
E_{1}
, the remaining
V-1
vertex energies have one-to-one correspondence with the
I=V-1
line energies. We will see this relation more explicitly below.

The structure of the series (
43
) tells us that, when writing down the CIS of a graph, all we need to decide is the maximal vertex energy. The relative sizes of other energies are irrelevant. However, this is going to change if we cut the CIS. For one thing, after cutting a diagram open, the subgraph that does not contain the maximal vertex energy
E_{1}
is clearly ignorant about
E_{1}
, and thus we have to pick up the (locally) maximal vertex energy
within
this subgraph to expand its CIS. More importantly, as we shall see, the cut of a graph is
directional
, in the sense that the factorized propagator is not symmetric with respect to the exchange of two endpoint energies. The direction is determined by comparing the two locally maximal vertex energies of the two subgraphs separated by the cut line. Thus, as we execute more cuts, we need more information about the ordering of vertex energies. In the end, when we cut all lines, we need to decide the relative sizes of vertex energies for the two endpoints of every line.

Therefore, our strategy is to choose a particular ordering for all vertex energies. Then, the structure of the CIS and the directions of all cuts will be determined, as will be detailed below. On the other hand, we do not require any orderings among line energies, but only require that they can be sent to zero so that we can access the signal branch point. For convenience of later presentation, we will make a working assumption that any line energy
K_{\alpha}
is smaller than its two endpoint vertex energies
E_{i}
and
E_{j}
. However, this assumption can be loosened in the final results. We will come back to this point later.

3.4
Relation to the Lauricella system

It is curious to observe that the differential equations for arbitrary massive trees (
14
) belong to a coupled Lauricella system
[
94
,
91
]
. We show this connection explicitly here. This subsection is independent of the rest of the paper, and uninterested readers can safely skip it.

Let us switch off the source term in (
14
) for the moment. As shown above, the solutions to these homogeneous equations correspond to the maximal-cut piece. From the viewpoint of bulk time integrals, this means that the completely homogeneous solutions involve no nested time integrals. As a result, we only need to consider a single time integral for each vertex, and the final result will be a product of these single-vertex integrals. In this sense, the completely homogeneous solution is the simplest part of the whole correlator.

The single time integral can be performed directly. Let’s consider the most general case where a single vertex with dimensionless time
z_{i}=E_{i}\tau_{i}
is attached to
N
internal lines with line energy
K_{\alpha}
and mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}
(
\alpha=1,\cdots,N
). After cutting all these lines, every line contributes to the
z_{i}
-integral by a massive mode function: (See (
3
)-(
5
).)

\sigma_{\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}}(r_{\alpha}z_{i})\propto(-r_{\alpha}z_{i})^{3/2}\text{H}_{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}}^{(j_{\alpha})}(-r_{\alpha}z_{i})

(44)

in which we have two choices
j_{\alpha}=1,2
for every line. Once we make a choice, it follows that
\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}=-(-1)^{j_{\alpha}}
. Also,
r_{\alpha}\equiv K_{\alpha}/E_{i}
. (
\alpha=1,\cdots,N
). Then, for example, if we choose all
j_{\alpha}=+1
, then the corresponding
z_{i}
-integral has the following form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(r_{1},\cdots,r_{N})=\mathcal{C}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{N}\Big[(-r_{\alpha}z_{i})^{3/2}\text{H}_{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\ell}}^{(1)}(-r_{\alpha}z_{i})\Big],

(45)

where we have included a constant coefficient
\mathcal{C}
that is not of interest here.

The integral (
45
) can be directly done by the partial Mellin-Barnes representation
[
76
,
58
]
. That is, we use the MB representation to rewrite all the massive mode functions (
44
). Taking
\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}=+
in (
44
) as an example, we have:

\displaystyle\sigma_{+}(r_{\alpha}z_{i})\propto\int_{-\mathrm{i}\infty}^{+\mathrm{i}\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}s_{\alpha}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{e^{(2s_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}-1)\pi\mathrm{i}/2}}\,}{\pi}\Gamma\Big[s_{\alpha}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}}\,}{2},s_{\alpha}-\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}}\,}{2}\Big]\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{-r_{\alpha}z_{i}}\,}{2}\Big)^{-2s_{\alpha}}(-r_{\alpha}z_{i})^{3/2}.

(46)

Then the time integral (
45
) can be easily performed, and the result is:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(r_{1},\cdots,r_{N})=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{C}\int_{-\mathrm{i}\infty}^{+\mathrm{i}\infty}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{N}\bigg\{\displaystyle\frac{\,{{\mathrm{d}}s_{\alpha}}\,}{2\pi\mathrm{i}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{e^{(2s_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}-1)\pi\mathrm{i}/2}}\,}{\pi}\Gamma\Big[s_{\alpha}+\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}}\,}{2},s_{\alpha}-\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}}\,}{2}\Big]\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{\alpha}}\,}{2}\Big)^{-2s_{\alpha}}r_{\alpha}^{3/2}\bigg\}

\displaystyle\times\Gamma(1+p_{i}+3N/2-2s_{1\cdots N})(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i})^{-1-p_{i}-3N/2+2s_{1\cdots N}},

(47)

Finally, the Mellin integral over all
s_{1},\cdots,s_{N}
can be finished by collecting the left poles, namely, setting
s_{\alpha}=-n_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}/2
with
\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}=\pm
, and we get a multivariate hypergeometric series:

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}(r_{1},\cdots,r_{N})=

\displaystyle~\mathcal{C}\sum_{\mathsf{c}_{1},\cdots,\mathsf{c}_{N}=\pm}\sum_{n_{1},\cdots,n_{N}=0}^{\infty}\Gamma\Big(1+p_{i}+3N/2+2n_{1\cdots N}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{T}}\Big)(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i})^{-1-p_{i}-3N/2-\mathrm{i}\nu_{\text{T}}}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\alpha=1}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{n_{\alpha}!}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}e^{(1+\mathsf{c}_{\alpha})\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}\pi/2}}\,}{\sin(\mathrm{i}\pi\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})\Gamma(1+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+n_{\alpha})}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{\alpha}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2n_{\alpha}+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}}r_{\alpha}^{3/2}.

(48)

Here
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\text{T}}\equiv\sum\limits_{\ell=1}^{N}\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}
. The choices of
\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}=\pm
(
\alpha=1,\cdots,N
) correspond to two leading falloffs of the scalar mode
\sigma_{\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}}
in the soft limit. We can recognize that all choices lead to Lauricella’s
\text{F}_{\text{C}}
function. For instance, if we choose all
\mathsf{c}_{\alpha}=+1
. Then the above series sums to the following function up to an overall constant factor:

\displaystyle\text{F}_{\text{C}}\left[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{i}+1}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{3N}\,}{4}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{T}}\,}{2}$},\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{i}+2}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{3N}\,}{4}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{T}}\,}{2}$}\\
1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\cdots,1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{N}\end{matrix}\middle|r_{1}^{2},\cdots,r_{N}^{2}\right]r_{1}^{3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\cdots r_{N}^{3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{N}}.

(49)

From this result, it is straightforward to see that the operators
\mathcal{D}_{(\alpha i)}
in our differential equation systems all belong to the Lauricella class
[
91
]
upon the change of variables
r_{i}^{2}\rightarrow u_{i}
. Thus, the differential equation systems can be viewed as a coupled Lauricella system.

4  Building Inhomogeneous Solution: Recursion Formulae

From this section onwards, we are going to solve the differential equation system for an arbitrary massive tree graph in the “signal region,” i.e., when all line energies are small compared to at least some of the vertex energies. As detailed in Sec.
3.3
, our strategy will be to solve the “most difficult” problem first, by working out the completely inhomogeneous solution (CIS). For this purpose, a very useful tool is a set of recursion formulae that allow us to directly build a series representation for the inhomogeneous solution with respect to a given line, in terms of the solution for a subgraph with the line removed. With these recursion formulae, we can build solutions for arbitrary trees from a 1-site graph simply by adding lines. In this section, we are going to derive these recursion formulae.

4.1
Ingoing recursion formula

In an arbitrary tree graph, we call an internal line a “leaf,” if this internal line is attached to a vertex not shared by any other internal lines. Then, it is a trivial observation that any tree diagram can be reduced to a 1-site graph by repetitively removing its “leaves.”

Figure 4:
Adding a new internal line (blue) with line energy
K
to an existing graph at Vertex
j
. The ingoing (left plot) and outgoing (right plot) lines correspond to the energy orderings
E_{i}>E_{j}
and
E_{i}<E_{j}
, respectively.

Suppose we have known a series expression for a
V
-site tree graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
, and we are going to find the series expression for a
(V+1)
-site graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}
, which is obtained by adding a leaf to
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
. To be specific, we show a general situation in Fig.
4
, in which we add a new (blue) line, with mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
and energy
K
, to Vertex
j
of an existing graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
. The two endpoints of the line are respectively attached to Vertices
i
and
j
, carrying vertex energies
E_{i}
and
E_{j}
, and twists
p_{i}
and
p_{j}
, respectively. As explained in Sec.
3.3
, it is important to distinguish between two cases with
E_{i}>E_{j}
and
E_{i}<E_{j}
, corresponding to the ingoing and outgoing lines in Fig.
4
. We consider the ingoing case in this subsection and the outgoing case in the next. In both cases, we will assume
K<\min\{E_{i},E_{j}\}
. While we only consider small
K_{\alpha}
regions in this work, it is possible to derive recursion formulae for large
K_{\alpha}
, which we will consider in a future work.

Another important point is that, before we add the leaf
K
to
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
, we take the twist parameter of Vertex
j
to be
p_{ij}+4
, so that
{\mathcal{G}}
can be treated as a valid source term of the differential equation (
14
).

In the most general situation, Vertex
j
of
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
is connected to
N\geq 0
lines with line energies
K_{k}
(
k=1,\cdots,N
). For convenience, we introduce the following notations for the momentum ratios, which are also shown in Fig.
4
:

\displaystyle r_{i}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{K}\,}{E_{i}},

\displaystyle r_{j}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{K}\,}{E_{j}},

\displaystyle r_{k}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{k}}\,}{E_{j}}.~~~(k=1,\cdots,N)

(50)

As assumed, we have an analytical expression for
{\mathcal{G}}
as series expansions in
2V-2
independent energy ratios, including
r_{1},\cdots,r_{N}
. The full series expression for
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
is irrelevant to us. All we need is the dependence on
r_{k}

(k=1,\cdots,N)
in this series. Without loss of generality, let us parameterize the series expression for
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
(with the twist of Vertex
j
being
p_{ij}+4
) as:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{V}\big|_{p_{j}\rightarrow p_{ij}+4}=\sum_{\{n\}}c_{\{n\}}r_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots r_{N}^{q_{N}}\times\cdots.

(51)

Here
\{n\}
is a collection of summation variables
n_{1},\cdots,n_{2V-2}
, taking values from all nonnegative integers, and
c_{\{n\}}
is a condensed notation for
c_{n_{1}\cdots n_{2V-2}}
. We spelled out the dependences on
r_{1},\cdots,r_{N}
, and dependences on all other
r
’s are suppressed. In general, the exponents
q_{1},\cdots,q_{N}
are simple functions of
n_{1},\cdots,n_{2V-2}
(
4
4

4

More precisely, from the form of the CIS in (
43
), we see that
q_{i}
has the form
q_{i}=a_{ij}n_{j}+b_{j}
, with
a_{ij}\in\{0,\pm 1,\pm 2\}
, and
b_{j}\in\mathbb{C}
. In addition, for a fixed
i
, there are at most two
a_{ij}
’s nonvanishing. The presence of
b_{j}
takes account of possible dependences on the twist or on the mass that arises from the cut.
). Also, the graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
is generally a sum of many pieces, each of which has a series expansion in the form of (
51
). Since our recursive algorithm is fully distributive over the summation of these pieces, it suffices to consider one piece at a time.

The strategy for finding
{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}
is to solve its differential equation with respect to
r_{i}
, which reads:

\displaystyle~\Big[\big(\vartheta_{i}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}\big)^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}-r_{i}^{2}(\vartheta_{i}+p_{i}+2)(\vartheta_{i}+p_{i}+1)\Big]{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}^{p_{j}+4}r_{j}^{p_{i}+4}}\,}{r_{ij}^{p_{ij}+5}}{\mathcal{G}}_{V}\big|_{E_{j}\rightarrow E_{ij},p_{j}\rightarrow p_{ij}+4}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}^{p_{j}+4}r_{j}^{p_{i}+4}}\,}{r_{ij}^{p_{ij}+5}}\sum_{\{n\}}c_{\{n\}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}r_{1}}\,}{r_{ij}}\Big)^{q_{1}}\cdots\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}r_{N}}\,}{r_{ij}}\Big)^{q_{N}}\times\cdots

(52)

Here we have used the fact that shifting
E_{j}\rightarrow E_{ij}
amounts to multiplying every
r_{k}

(k=1,\cdots,k_{N})
by a factor of
r_{i}/r_{ij}
.

The equation in (
52
) is a standard second-order ordinary differential equation with a source term. So its solution is given by the sum of an inhomogeneous part and a homogeneous part. The inhomogeneous part is solved with the source term, while the homogeneous part is in general a linear combination of two independent solutions, with the coefficients determined by appropriate boundary conditions. We will find the inhomogeneous solution below, and leave the homogeneous solution to Sec.
6
.

Expansion in small
r_{i}

The presence of
r_{ij}
in (
52
) makes the source term troublesome. So, we Taylor expand it. The way to expand it depends on the form of the final series. Since we are interested in the region
E_{i}>E_{j}
, namely,
r_{i}
being small, we should look for an inhomogeneous solution expanded in
r_{i}
and
r_{i}/r_{j}
. So, we expand
r_{ij}
factors in (
52
) in the following way:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{r_{ij}^{p_{ij}+q_{1\cdots N}+5}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{r_{j}^{p_{ij}+q_{1\cdots N}+5}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell}(\ell+1)_{p_{ij}+q_{1\cdots N}+4}}\,}{\Gamma(p_{ij}+q_{1\cdots N}+5)}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{r_{j}}\Big)^{\ell}.

(53)

Then the source term in (
52
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}^{p_{j}+4}r_{j}^{p_{i}+4}}\,}{r_{ij}^{p_{ij}+5}}{\mathcal{G}}_{V}\big|_{E_{j}\rightarrow E_{ij},p_{j}\rightarrow p_{ij}+4}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\{n\}}\tilde{c}_{\ell\{n\}}\,r_{i}^{3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{r_{j}}\Big)^{\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}+1}r_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots r_{N}^{q_{N}}\times\cdots,

(54)

with the following coefficient:

\displaystyle\tilde{c}_{\ell\{n\}}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell}(\ell+1)_{p_{ij}+q_{1\cdots N}+4}}\,}{\Gamma(p_{ij}+q_{1\cdots N}+5)}c_{\{n\}}.

(55)

Given the form of the source term (
54
), we can try the following ansatz for the solution inhomogeneous (Inh) with respect to Line
K
:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{Inh}}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}\big]=\sum_{\ell,m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\{n\}}d_{\ell m\{n\}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2m+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{r_{j}}\Big)^{\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}+1}r_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots r_{N}^{q_{N}}\times\cdots.

(56)

Here we have included a factor of
1/2
in
(r_{i}/2)^{2m+3}
for later convenience.
Substituting this ansatz into (
52
) and matching the powers of
r_{i}
on both sides of the equation, we get a set of recursion relations. In particular, matching the
\mathcal{O}({r_{i}^{\ell+p_{j}+q_{1\cdots N}+4}})
terms, we get an “initial condition:”

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{4}\Big[\big(\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}\big)^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}\Big]d_{\ell 0\{n\}}=2\tilde{c}_{\ell\{n\}}.

(57)

Then, matching
\mathcal{O}({r_{i}^{2m+\ell+p_{j}+q_{1\cdots N}+4}})
terms gives the recursion formula:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{4}\Big[\big(2m+\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}\big)^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}\Big]d_{\ell m\{n\}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~(2m+\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+4)(2m+\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+3)d_{\ell(m-1)\{n\}}.

(58)

It is straightforward to find a general-term formula for the coefficients
d_{\ell m\{n\}}
by combining (
57
) and (
58
):
