\displaystyle d_{\ell m\{n\}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{2(\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+5)_{2m}}\,}{\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m+1}}\tilde{c}_{\ell\{n\}}.

(59)

Here and below, we introduce the shorthand notations for the product of a pair of Pochhammer symbols:

\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m+1}\equiv\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m+1}\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m+1}.

(60)

Then, using (
55
) to replace
\tilde{c}_{\ell\{n\}}
by the original (known) coefficient
c_{\{n\}}
, we finally get a main result of this subsection:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{Inh}}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}\big]=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\{n\}}d_{\ell m\{n\}}\,\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2m+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{r_{j}}\Big)^{\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}+1}r_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots r_{N}^{q_{N}}\times\cdots;

(61)

\displaystyle d_{\ell m\{n\}}=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{2(-1)^{\ell}(q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+5)_{\ell+2m}}\,}{\ell!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m+1}}c_{\{n\}}.

(62)

That is, we have found an inhomogeneous solution to the
r_{i}
-equation (
52
), expressed as a series (
56
), whose coefficients
d_{\ell m\{n\}}
are obtained by a simple “dressing” of the series coefficients of
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
. This formula works for
E_{i}>E_{j}
so that the newly added line is ingoing. So, we call it the
ingoing recursion formula
.

Consistency check

We have found the inhomogeneous solution to the
r_{i}
-equation alone by considering a series ansatz. This is not quite enough to claim that the series (
61
)
is
the inhomogeneous part of the full diagram
{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}
, because, for that purpose, we must check that the expression (
61
) satisfies
all
of
2V
differential equations for
{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}
besides the
r_{i}
-equation.

The
2V-N-1
differential equations for
r
variables not explicitly shown in (
50
) are trivially satisfied by
[{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}]_{\text{inh}}
in (
61
), if the “old” graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
satisfies the corresponding equations. The only nontrivial equations to be checked are those associated with
r
variables involving Vertex
j
, namely
r_{j}
and all
r_{k}
(
k=1,\cdots,N
).

Let’s first check the
r_{j}
-equation. Acting the operator
\mathcal{D}_{r_{j}}
on the ansatz (
56
), we have:

\displaystyle\Big[(\vartheta_{r_{j}}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$})+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}-r_{j}^{2}(\vartheta_{j1\cdots N}+p_{j}+2)(\vartheta_{j1\cdots N}+p_{j}+1)\Big]\mathop{\text{Inh}}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\{n\}}d_{\ell m\{n\}}\Big[\big(\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$}\big)^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}-r_{j}^{2}\ell(\ell-1)\Big]

\displaystyle\times\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2m+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{r_{j}}\Big)^{\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}+1}r_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots r_{N}^{q_{N}}\times\cdots.

(63)

Here
\vartheta_{j1\cdots N}\equiv\vartheta_{j}+\vartheta_{1}+\cdots+\vartheta_{N}
. The result of
\mathcal{D}_{r_{j}}\mathop{\text{Inh}}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}\big]
is again given by (
54
). Therefore, we can match terms of equal powers of
r_{i}
or
r_{j}
between (
63
) and (
54
). In particular, matching at
\mathcal{O}({r_{i}^{4+\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}}})
yields the same “initial condition” (
57
). On the other hand, matching terms of equal power of
r_{j}
, we get a new recursion relation for the coefficients
d_{\ell m\{n\}}
:

\displaystyle d_{\ell m\{n\}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{4(\ell+2)(\ell+1)}\,}{(\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$})^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}d_{(\ell+2)(m-1)\{n\}}.

(64)

One can easily check that this recursion relation is satisfied by our series solution with (
62
). Thus the series (
61
) is indeed a solution to the
r_{j}
-equation.

Next, we check the
r_{1}
-equation. The procedures for the rest of
r_{k}
-equations with
k=2,\cdots,N
are identical. The original
V
-site graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
satisfies a similar set of
r_{k}
-equations with
k=1,\cdots,N
, but these equations are modified after including the new line. Thus we need to check them.

Now, acting
\mathcal{D}_{r_{1}}
operator on
\mathop{\text{Inh}}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}\big]
, we get:

\displaystyle\Big[(\vartheta_{r_{1}}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$})^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}^{2}-r_{1}^{2}(\vartheta_{j1\cdots N}+p_{j}+2)(\vartheta_{j1\cdots N}+p_{j}+1)\Big]\mathop{\text{Inh}}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\{n\}}d_{\ell m\{n\}}\Big[\big(q_{1}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}\big)^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}^{2}-r_{1}^{2}\ell(\ell-1)\Big]

\displaystyle\times\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2m+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{r_{j}}\Big)^{\ell+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{j}+1}r_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots r_{N}^{q_{N}}\times\cdots

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle~0~\text{or a $V$-site graph}.

(65)

The result of
\mathcal{D}_{r_{1}}\mathop{\text{Inh}}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}\big]
depends on which types of series we are picking up for
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
in (
51
). In any case, by matching powers of energy ratios, we get a recursion relation:

\displaystyle d_{\ell m\{n(q_{1})\}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{(\ell+2)(\ell+1)}\,}{(q_{1}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$})^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}^{2}}d_{(\ell+2)m\{n(q_{1}-2)\}}.

(66)

Here by
\{n(q_{1})\}
we are highlighting that some of the summation variables
n_{1},\cdots,n_{2V-2}
depend on the power
q_{1}
, and here we are forming a recursion relation for two
d_{\ell m\{n(q_{1})\}}
’s where
q_{1}
jumps by 2. Given Footnote
4
, this normally involves some summation variables in
\{n\}
jump by 1 or 2. We will see more explicit examples in the next section.

On the other hand, we note that the coefficient
c_{\{n\}}
for the series (
51
) of the original subgraph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
satisfies another recursion relation coming from the
r_{1}
-equation for the subgraph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
:

\displaystyle\Big[(\vartheta_{1}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$})^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}^{2}-r_{1}^{2}(\vartheta_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+6)(\vartheta_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+5)\Big]{\mathcal{G}}_{V}=0~\text{or a $(V-1)$-site graph}.

(67)

Here we have used the facts that the mass for Line
K_{1}
(shown in Fig.
4
) is
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}
, and that the twist for Vertex
j
in the original graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
is
p_{ij}+4
. Again, there are two possibilities for the right-hand side, depending on whether we consider the inhomogeneous or homogeneous piece with respect to Line
K_{1}
. Importantly, the recursion relation is independent of the right-hand side being zero or not. In both cases, we have:

\displaystyle c_{\{n(q_{1})\}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{(q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+4)(q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+3)}\,}{(q_{1}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$})^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}^{2}}c_{\{n(q_{1}-2)\}}.

(68)

Now it is trivial to check that the recursion relation (
66
) is satisfied by our solution (
62
). Explicitly, (
62
) tells us the following relation:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{d_{\ell m\{n(q_{1})\}}}\,}{d_{(\ell+2)m\{n(q_{1}-2)\}}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{(\ell+2)(\ell+1)}\,}{(q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+4)(q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+3)}\displaystyle\frac{\,{c_{\{n(q)\}}}\,}{c_{\{n(q-2)\}}}.

(69)

Then, substituting (
68
) into this relation, we immediately get (
66
). This shows that our series for
\mathop{\text{Inh}}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}\big]
with coefficients (
62
) is indeed a valid solution for
all
differential equations for new graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}
.

4.2
Outgoing recursion formula

Now we repeat the derivation for the inhomogeneous part of the
(V+1)
-site graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}
above, but this time consider the other energy ordering
E_{i}<E_{j}
. This corresponds to the right graph of Fig.
4
. In this region, we need to develop a different expansion, in terms of
r_{j}
and
r_{j}/r_{i}
.

As one can anticipate, it is easier to use the
r_{j}
-equation to generate the solution. After that, we will check the consistency of this solution with other equations. The
r_{j}
-equation reads:

\Big[(\vartheta_{r_{j}}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$})^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}-r_{j}^{2}(\vartheta_{j1\cdots N}+p_{j}+2)(\vartheta_{j1\cdots N}+p_{j}+1)\Big]{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}^{p_{j}+4}r_{j}^{p_{i}+4}}\,}{r_{ij}^{p_{ij}+5}}{\mathcal{G}}_{V}\big|_{E_{j}\rightarrow E_{ij},p_{j}\rightarrow p_{ij}+4}.

(70)

The source term is identical to the
r_{i}
-equation. However, we need to expand powers of
r_{ij}
in the small
r_{j}/r_{i}
limit:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{r_{ij}^{p_{ij}+q_{1\cdots N}+5}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{r_{i}^{p_{ij}+q_{1\cdots N}+5}}\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell}(\ell+1)_{p_{ij}+q_{1\cdots N}+4}}\,}{\Gamma(p_{ij}+q_{1\cdots N}+5)}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}}\,}{r_{i}}\Big)^{\ell}.

(71)

Correspondingly, the source term can be expanded as:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}^{p_{j}+4}r_{j}^{p_{i}+4}}\,}{r_{ij}^{p_{ij}+5}}{\mathcal{G}}_{V}\big|_{E_{j}\rightarrow E_{ij},p_{j}\rightarrow p_{ij}+4}=\sum_{\ell=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\{n\}}\tilde{c}_{\ell\{n\}}\,r_{j}^{3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}}\,}{r_{i}}\Big)^{\ell+p_{i}+1}r_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots r_{N}^{q_{N}}\times\cdots,

(72)

where the coefficients
\tilde{c}_{\ell\{n\}}
are still given by (
55
).

The source term (
72
) suggests to us the following ansatz for the inhomogeneous part of the new graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}
:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{Inh}}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}\big]=\sum_{\ell,m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\{n\}}b_{\ell m\{n\}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2m+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}}\,}{r_{i}}\Big)^{\ell+p_{i}+1}r_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots r_{N}^{q_{N}}\times\cdots.

(73)

Again, we included a factor of
1/2
in
(r_{j}/2)^{2m+3}
for later convenience.
Then, the procedure of determining the coefficients
b_{\ell m\{n\}}
is identical to the previous case, and so we will be brief. First, we substitute the ansatz (
73
) into the
r_{j}
-equation (
70
), which gives:

\displaystyle\Big[(\vartheta_{r_{j}}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$})^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}-r_{j}^{2}(\vartheta_{j1\cdots N}+p_{j}+2)(\vartheta_{j1\cdots N}+p_{j}+1)\Big]\mathop{\text{Inh}}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}\big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m,\{n\}}b_{\ell m\{n\}}\Big[(\ell+2m+p_{i}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$})^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}-r_{j}^{2}(\ell+2m+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+6)(\ell+2m+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+5)\Big]

\displaystyle~\times\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2m+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}}\,}{r_{i}}\Big)^{\ell+p_{i}+1}r_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots r_{N}^{q_{N}}\times\cdots.

(74)

Then, matching the coefficients of
\mathcal{O}({r_{j}^{\ell+p_{i}+4}})
terms on the both sides, we get an “initial condition:”

b_{\ell 0\{n\}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{8\tilde{c}_{\ell\{n\}}}\,}{(\ell+p_{i}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$})^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}.

(75)

Matching coefficients of
\mathcal{O}({r_{j}^{\ell+2m+p_{i}+4}})
terms, we get the recursion relation:

b_{\ell m\{n\}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{4(\ell+2m+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+4)(\ell+2m+q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+3)}\,}{(\ell+2m+p_{i}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$})^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}b_{\ell(m-1)\{n\}}.

(76)

From the above result, together with (
55
), it is again straightforward to find the following
outgoing recursion formula
:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{Inh}}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}\big]=

\displaystyle~\sum_{\ell,m=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\{n\}}b_{\ell m\{n\}}\,\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2m+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}}\,}{r_{i}}\Big)^{\ell+p_{i}+1}r_{1}^{q_{1}}\cdots r_{N}^{q_{N}}\times\cdots,

(77)

\displaystyle b_{\ell m\{n\}}=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{2(-1)^{\ell}(q_{1\cdots N}+p_{ij}+5)_{\ell+2m}}\,}{\ell!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell+p_{i}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m+1}}c_{\{n\}}.

(78)

This formula gives the inhomogeneous part (with respect to the new line
K
) of the new graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V+1}
in terms of the result of the old graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
, and holds when
E_{i}<E_{j}
.

Once again, we cannot claim (
77
) is the right formula unless we have checked that it also solves the
r_{i}
-equation and all
r_{k}
-equation with
k=1,\cdots,N
. Going through the same steps as before, it is easy to see that the
r_{i}
-equation leads to the following recursion relations for the coefficients
b_{\ell m\{n\}}
:

\displaystyle b_{\ell m\{n\}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{4(\ell+1)(\ell+2)}\,}{(\ell+p_{i}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{2}$})^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{2}}b_{(\ell+2)(m-1)\{n\}},

(79)

while the
r_{1}
-equation leads to the following recursion relation:

\displaystyle b_{\ell m\{n(q_{1})\}}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{(\ell+2m+p_{ij}+q_{1\cdots N}+4)(\ell+2m+p_{ij}+q_{1\cdots N}+3)}\,}{(q_{1}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$})^{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}^{2}}b_{\ell m\{n(q_{1}-2)\}}.

(80)

It is straightforward to verify that our solution (
78
) satisfies both recursion relations. Clearly, the other
r_{k}
-equations (
k=2,\cdots,N
) can be checked in a way very similar to
r_{1}
-equation, and we will not elaborate on them.

To finish this section, we note that the ingoing recursion formula (
61
) is convergent when both
r_{i}=K/E_{i}
and
r_{i}/r_{j}=E_{j}/E_{i}
are small. The outgoing formula (
77
) works in the other direction, when
r_{j}=K/E_{j}
and
r_{j}/r_{i}
are both small. It may be useful to note that the outgoing formula is not necessarily the analytical continuation of the ingoing formula in the small-
r_{j}/r_{i}
region. This is evident when applying these recursion formulae to build a 2-site graph from a 1-site graph. In that case, if we analytically continue the result from the ingoing formula to the region where the outgoing formula is supposed to work, we will get a piece identical to the outgoing formula. However, we will also get another piece that is nonanalytic in the vertex energy ratios, which is actually a part of the local signal. However, in certain cases, applying the ingoing and outgoing formulae gives identical results. We will see examples in the next section. Indeed, it is this “happy coincidence” that allows us to find a simple formula for the CIS of arbitrary graphs with arbitrary vertex energy orderings.

5  Completely Inhomogeneous Solution

In this section, we make use of the recursion formulae of the previous section to work out the CIS of general massive tree correlators. We will again work with the dimensionless version (
11
).

Recall that the recursion formulae derived in the previous section are quite general in the following sense: When we add a new line to a given graph, the recursion formulae give the inhomogeneous piece of the new graph with respect to the newly added line. The given graph consists of many pieces corresponding to the homogeneous or inhomogeneous solutions to all of its differential operators. The recursion formulae work for each of these pieces individually. Thus we can use these formulae to construct a graph recursively. That is, we add a line to a single-vertex graph and use the recursion formulae to build the 2-site inhomogeneous solution, and then use boundary conditions to fix the corresponding homogeneous solution. This gives the full result of a two-vertex graph. Then, we add one more line to the full two-vertex solution to build a three-vertex graph, and repeat this procedure until we reach the final
V
-vertex graph that we want to compute.

However, it turns out more streamlined to neglect all homogeneous solutions and focus only on the completely inhomogeneous part, which is traditionally considered as the most difficult part of the problem. Then, the homogeneous solutions can be obtained by applying simple cuts as will be detailed in Sec.
6
. Below, we carry out this recursive construction of the CIS. It turns out that the resulting expression has a nice interpretation as a direct generalization of the family tree integrals introduced in
[
80
,
68
]
. This massive version of family trees also turns out to be useful when we construct homogeneous solutions below.

5.1
Starting point: graph ordering

Our starting point is a general massive tree correlator of
V
vertices, described by
V
vertex energies
E_{1},\cdots,E_{V}
and
V-1
line energies
K_{2},\cdots,K_{V}
. To construct a series solution, we must specialize in a particular kinematic region where our final series expression is expected to be convergent, as explained in Sec.
3.3
. For our purpose of constructing the CIS, we only need a very mild prescription: We only require that the largest energies among all
2V-1
variables
\{E_{i},K_{j}\}
is a
vertex energy
. Without loss of generality, we will just call it
E_{1}
. All other
2V-2
variables
E_{i},K_{i}
(
i=2,\cdots,V
) can have arbitrary ordering, as long as they are all suitably smaller than
E_{1}
.

Once we impose a particular order to all vertex energies, say
E_{1}>E_{i_{2}}>\cdots>E_{i_{V}}
, the graph acquires a directional structure. (Here
(i_{2},\cdots,i_{V})
can be an arbitrary permutation of
(2,\cdots,V)
.) Consequently, to every internal line, we can add a direction which points from the vertex of larger energy towards the vertex of smaller energy. We illustrate this energy direction with an example in the left graph of Fig.
5
. We will call this directional structure the
imposed order
since it is imposed from our choice of the kinematic region
E_{1}>E_{i_{2}}>\cdots>E_{i_{V}}
.

Figure 5:
An illustration of graph orderings.
Left: The direction shows the imposed order obtained by comparing the relative sizes of all adjacent vertex energies. The maximal energy vertex (Vertex 1) is marked with a blue dot, the correct lines are colored in blue and the wrong lines in red. Middle: The induced order. Right: The imposed family tree (namely, the maximal connected correct subgraph containing the maximal energy site).

On the other hand, it is crucial to introduce the concept of a partially ordered graph. A partial order means that, to any vertex, we can attach any number of lines with out-going directions, but at most one line with in-going direction. To borrow the convenient nomenclature of
[
80
,
68
]
, for any line connecting two vertices, we call the vertex with larger energy the
mother
, and the vertex with smaller energy the
daughter
. Then a partial order means that a mother can have any number of daughters but a daughter can have at most one mother. Consequently, a partially ordered graph naturally acquires an interpretation as a maternal family tree, which explains our use of this terminology.

With this background knowledge, it is now clear that, once we pick up the largest vertex energy
E_{1}
, there is a unique partial order of the whole graph that is naturally induced by
E_{1}
, in which
E_{1}
acts as the ancestor of the whole family tree. We call it the
induced partial order
, to distinguish it from the imposed order introduced above. We show the induced order in the middle graph of Fig.
5
.

In general, the imposed order is not a partial order, and is different from the induced partial order. So the two orderings agree on some internal lines and conflict on others. For later convenience, we call internal lines with agreed orders “correct lines,” and lines with conflicting orders “wrong lines.” Correct and wrong lines are respectively colored in blue and red in the left plot of Fig.
5
. By construction, all lines attached to the maximal energy
E_{1}
must be correct lines. These correct lines can be extended to a maximal partially ordered graph starting from
E_{1}
, which is in general a subgraph of the whole graph. We call it the
imposed family tree
of
E_{1}
, as shown in the right plot of Fig.
5
.

With the above preparation, we can carry out the recursive construction of the CIS. Our procedure is the following:

1.

We first consider the imposed family tree, and work backward from the last generation to earlier generations. After this step, we will get a formula for CIS for a partially order graph.

2.

To make further progress, we need to add a “wrong line” to the imposed family tree. We will show that the resulting expression for the CIS after adding a wrong line is the
same
as adding a “correct line.” Thus, the directions of all wrong lines can be reversed, so that the induced partial order actually works for the whole graph.

3.

As a result, the formula we derived for the partially ordered graph actually works when the imposed order is not a partial order, and this finishes our derivation of the completely inhomogeneous solution.

We show this procedure with an example in Fig.
6
.

Figure 6:
The strategy for recursively constructing the CIS of an arbitrary tree with arbitrary vertex energy orderings. (1) By using the outgoing recursion formula, we can find the CIS for a two-generation subfamily. (2) We then add one ingoing line to enlarge the graph to one earlier generation. (3) The above two steps allow us to construct the CIS for arbitrary partially ordered graph. Thus we reach the result for the imposed family tree. (4) We then add one wrong line to the imposed family tree, and show that it is in fact equivalent to a correct line. (5) Using the results of Step 4, we know how to add lines of arbitrary directions, and thus can complete the construction for the CIS of the whole graph.

5.2
Recursive construction of CIS

Building one generation

As outlined above, we first consider the imposed family tree, starting from the last generation. Thus, in this subsection, we work with a vertex with energy
E_{1}
in a second-to-last generation in the imposed family tree, together with all her daughters. Suppose the site
E_{1}
has
N-1
daughters with energies
E_{2},\cdots,E_{N}
, as shown in Fig.
7
. Also, we assume that the line connecting
E_{1}
and
E_{i}
carries the line energy
K_{i}
and mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}
(
i=2,\cdots,N
), to be in accordance with our general rule of labeling a massive family tree. (See below and App.
A.2
.) They together form an
N
-site star graph, with all lines pointing outwards. Below, we construct the CIS of this
N
-site star graph by recursively adding new lines to a single vertex
E_{1}
.

Figure 7:
The first step of recursively constructing the CIS: the
N
-site star graph
\big\llbracket{1(2)\cdots(N)}\big\rrbracket
.

First, the one-vertex graph with vertex energy
E_{1}
and twist
p_{1}
can be trivially computed as:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket=E_{1}^{p_{1}+1}\sum_{\mathsf{a}=\pm}\int_{-\infty}^{0}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\,\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}(-\tau)^{p_{1}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}E_{1}\tau}=2\cos(\pi p_{1}/2)\Gamma(p_{1}+1).

(81)

From this point onwards, we introduce our notations for “massive family trees,” reviewed in App.
A.2
(
5
5

5

The massive family trees
\big\llbracket{\cdots}\big\rrbracket
in App.
A.2
are defined via bulk time integrals, while we are using the recursion relations to build the same objects in this subsection. The reason that the two approaches give the same result is discussed below (
102
).
). Here,
\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket
simply means (the CIS of) the one-site graph.

Now, we want to add one line connecting
E_{1}
with
E_{2}
, with line energy
K_{2}
and mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}
. Since
E_{2}<E_{1}
by construction, we should use the outgoing recursion formula (
77
). The recursion formula requires us to shift the twist
p_{1}
of the 1-vertex graph by
p_{1}\rightarrow p_{12}+4
. The shifted 1-vertex graph is:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket_{p_{1}\rightarrow p_{12}+4}=2\cos(\pi p_{12}/2)\Gamma(p_{12}+5).

(82)

Then, we get the CIS for the 2-site graph by directly applying the recursion formula (
77
):

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{12}\big\rrbracket=\sum_{\ell_{2},m_{2}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{4(-1)^{\ell_{2}}\cos(\pi p_{12}/2)\Gamma(\ell_{2}+2m_{2}+p_{12}+5)}\,}{\ell_{2}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{2}+p_{2}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{2}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{2}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{2}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{\ell_{2}+p_{2}+1}.

(83)

The summation variables
(\ell_{2},m_{2})
are chosen for easier generalization to
N
-site star in the following.

Similarly, we add one more line to
E_{1}
of
\big\llbracket{12}\big\rrbracket
to form the CIS of a 3-site graph
\big\llbracket{1(2)(3)}\big\rrbracket
. Here the notation means that Site 1 has two daughters 2 and 3. (See App.
A.2
.) Following the recursion method, we first shift the twist
p_{1}
in
\big\llbracket{12}\big\rrbracket
to get
\big\llbracket{12}\big\rrbracket_{p_{1}\rightarrow p_{13}+4}
, and then apply the recursion formula (
77
). The result is:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1(2)(3)}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{2},\ell_{3},m_{2},m_{3}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{4(-1)^{\ell_{2}}\cos(\pi p_{123}/2)\Gamma(\ell_{2}+2m_{2}+p_{123}+9)}\,}{\ell_{2}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{2}+p_{2}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{2}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}2m_{2}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{2}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\ell_{2}+p_{2}+1}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{2(-1)^{\ell_{3}}({\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}(\ell_{2}+2m_{2}+p_{2}+4)}+p_{13}+5)_{2m_{3}+\ell_{3}}}\,}{\ell_{3}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{3}+p_{3}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{3}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{3}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{3}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{3}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{\ell_{3}+p_{3}+1},

(84)

where we have highlighted the powers of
K_{2}/E_{1}
and
E_{2}/E_{1}
, which play the roles of
q_{1}
and
q_{2}
in the recursion formula (
77
). The above expression can be directly simplified to:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1(2)(3)}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{2},\ell_{3},m_{2},m_{3}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{8(-1)^{\ell_{2}}\cos(\pi p_{123}/2)\Gamma(\ell_{23}+2m_{23}+p_{123}+9)}\,}{\ell_{2}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{2}+p_{2}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{2}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{2}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{2}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{\ell_{2}+p_{2}+1}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{3}}}\,}{\ell_{3}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{3}+p_{3}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{3}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{3}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{3}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{3}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{\ell_{3}+p_{3}+1}.

(85)

We can repeat this procedure
N-1
times to get the CIS for the
N
-site star graph, as shown in Fig.
7
. It is not hard to see that the result is:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1(2)\cdots(N)}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}2^{N}\cos(\pi p_{1\cdots N}/2)\Gamma(\ell_{2\cdots N}+2m_{2\cdots N}+p_{1\cdots N}+4N-3)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=2}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{i}}}\,}{\ell_{i}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{i}+p_{i}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{i}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{i}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{i}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+p_{i}+1},

(86)

where we have abbreviated the summation sign:

\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}\equiv\sum_{\ell_{2},\cdots,\ell_{N}=0}^{\infty}\sum_{m_{2},\cdots,m_{N}=0}^{\infty}.

(87)

We will use similar abbreviated notations below, and the range of the
\{\ell,m\}
can be easily identified by inspecting the summand. With (
86
), we have completed the first step (Fig.
6
(1)) of constructing the CIS.

Adding more generations

Now we can go one generation earlier, and add the mother site of
E_{1}
, which we call
E_{0}
. Thus we need to add an ingoing line to the
E_{1}
site, as shown in Fig.
8
. As we shall see, adding ingoing lines introduces new features to the result, and is a bit subtler than adding outgoing lines.

Figure 8:
The second step of recursively constructing the CIS: the
N
-site star graph with one more generation added:
\big\llbracket{01(2)\cdots(N)}\big\rrbracket
.

We start from the
N
-site star in (
86
), and shift the twist
p_{1}
by
p_{1}\rightarrow p_{01}+4
. Then, we apply the ingoing recursion formula (
61
) as:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{01(2)\cdots(N)}\big\rrbracket=\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}2^{N+1}\cos(\pi p_{01\cdots N}/2)\Gamma(\ell_{2\cdots N}+2m_{2\cdots N}+p_{01\cdots N}+4N+1)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=2}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{i}}}\,}{\ell_{i}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{i}+p_{i}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{i}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{i}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{i}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+p_{i}+1}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{1}}(q_{1}+p_{01}+5)_{2m_{1}+\ell_{1}}}\,}{\ell_{1}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{1}+{q_{1}}+p_{1}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{1}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{1}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{1}+q_{1}+p_{1}+1},

(88)

Here we introduced a new parameter:

q_{1}\equiv\ell_{2\cdots N}+2m_{2\cdots N}+p_{2\cdots N}+4N-4,

(89)

which simply counts the dimension of
1/E_{1}
in the series before adding the factors for the new line, as required by the ingoing recursion formula. Note in particular that this
q_{1}
parameter appears in the exponent of the newly added
E_{1}/E_{0}
term. As such, it has the effect of replacing all
E_{1}
factors by
E_{0}
in the original
N
-site star graph, namely, the second line of (
88
). Thus the above expression can be further massaged into the following one:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{01(2)\cdots(N)}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}2^{N+1}\cos(\pi p_{01\cdots N}/2)\Gamma(q_{0}+p_{0}+1)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=1}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{i}}}\,}{\ell_{i}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{i}+q_{i}+p_{i}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{i}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{i}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{i}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+p_{i}+1}.

(90)

Here we have introduced more
q_{i}
parameters (
i=0,1,\cdots,N
). Besides
q_{1}
defined above, we have:

\displaystyle q_{0}\equiv q_{1}+\ell_{1}+2m_{1}+p_{1}+4,

\displaystyle q_{i}\equiv 0.~~(i=2,\cdots,N)

(91)

It is now obvious that we can continue this procedure through all generations of a family tree: Adding a daughter is straightforward, as shown above. On the other hand, when we add a new mother vertex
E_{i}
, we replace all vertex energies in the denominator of the existing graph with
E_{i}
. At the same time, we introduce a parameter
q_{i}
, defined in the following way:

q_{i}\equiv\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\ell\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}+2\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mum\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mup\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}+4N_{i},

(92)

where
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\ell\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}
means the sum of all
\ell_{j}
’s over the descendant vertices of Vertex
i
.
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mum\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}
and
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mup\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}
are defined similarly, and
N_{i}
is the number of descendant vertices of Vertex
i
. We call
q_{i}
the
family parameter
, since it encodes the family-tree structure of Vertex
i
’s descendants. Then, the expression (
90
) is equally applicable to all partially ordered graphs, and in particular, to our imposed trees. So, let us put it in a more general way:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{\mathscr{P}(\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu1\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu2\cdots N)}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}2^{N}\cos(\pi p_{1\cdots N}/2)\Gamma(q_{1}+p_{1}+1)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=2}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{i}}}\,}{\ell_{i}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{i}+q_{i}+p_{i}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{i}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{i}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{i}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+p_{i}+1},

(93)

where
\mathscr{P}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu1\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu2\cdots N)
means an arbitrary given partial order of the
N
sites, with
E_{1}
acting as the maximal vertex energy. This completes our recursive construction of the imposed family tree in the whole graph, namely Fig.
6
(3).

Adding wrong lines

At this point we have the expression for the CIS of the imposed tree. To enlarge this to the full graph, we need to include the rest of lines, and some of them have “wrong” directions. (Step (4) and (5) in Fig.
6
.) So, let us first consider the case of adding one wrong-direction line to any vertex of the imposed tree graph. Since the imposed tree is partially ordered, each of its vertices must have at most one ingoing line but can have any number of outgoing lines. Thus, the most general situation is to add the wrong-direction line to a vertex of the existing imposed tree, which has 1 ingoing line and
N-1
outgoing lines with
N\geq 1
. (The case of adding an ingoing line to a vertex without ingoing lines is nothing but
\big\llbracket{01(2\cdots N)}\big\rrbracket
considered above.)

Figure 9:
The fourth step of recursively constructing the CIS: the
N
-site star graph with one more generation and one wrong line (Left), whose expression is identical to the one with the wrong line reversed (Right).

Therefore, we can begin with the three-generation tree
\big\llbracket{01(2)\cdots(N)}\big\rrbracket
in (
90
), and add a new line with vertex energy
E_{A}
and line energy
K_{A}
to the
E_{1}
site. The “wrong direction” means
E_{A}>E_{1}
, and at this point, we do not assume any ordering between
E_{0}
and
E_{A}
. Then, following the same procedure as above, we first shift the twist of the
E_{1}
site in
\big\llbracket{01(2)\cdots(N)}\big\rrbracket
by
p_{1}\rightarrow p_{1A}+4
, and apply the ingoing recursion formula (
61
)-(
62
). Then we get:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{01(2)\cdots(N)(\overleftarrow{A})}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}2^{N+1}\cos(\pi p_{01\cdots NA}/2)\Gamma(\ell_{12\cdots N}+2m_{12\cdots N}+p_{012\cdots NA}+4N+5)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=2}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{i}}}\,}{\ell_{i}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{i}+p_{i}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{i}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{i}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{i}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+p_{i}+1}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{1}}}\,}{\ell_{1}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{1}+q_{1}+(p_{1A}+4}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{1}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{1}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{1}+p_{1A}+5}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{2(-1)^{\ell_{A}}(-\ell_{1})_{2m_{A}+\ell_{A}}}\,}{\ell_{A}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{A}-\ell_{1}-p_{A}}\,}{2}$}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{A}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{A}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{A}}\,}{2E_{A}}\Big)^{2m_{A}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{1}}\,}{E_{A}}\Big)^{\ell_{A}-\ell_{1}-p_{A}-4},

(94)

in which, for clarity, we have restored
q_{0}
and write out the
i=1
factor explicitly. Here, we encounter a new phenomenon due to the factor
(-\ell_{1})_{2m_{A}+\ell_{A}}=(-1)^{\ell_{A}}\ell_{1}!/\Gamma(1+\ell_{1}-2m_{A}-\ell_{A})
in the last line. This factor is zero unless
\ell_{1}\geq 2m_{A}+\ell_{A}
, and it suggests that we should shift the summation variable
\ell_{1}\rightarrow\ell_{1}+\ell_{A}+2m_{A}
. After this shift, we get:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{01(2)\cdots(N)(\overleftarrow{A})}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}2^{N+2}\cos(\pi p_{01\cdots NA}/2)\Gamma(\ell_{12\cdots NA}+2m_{12\cdots NA}+p_{012\cdots NA}+4N+5)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=2}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{i}}}\,}{\ell_{i}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{i}+p_{i}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{i}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{i}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{i}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+p_{i}+1}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{1}}}\,}{\ell_{1}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{1A}+2m_{A}+q_{1}+p_{1A}+4}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{1}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{1}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{1A}+2m_{A}+p_{1A}+5}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{A}}}\,}{\ell_{A}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{-\ell_{1}-2m_{A}-p_{A}}\,}{2}$}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{A}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{A}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{A}}\,}{2E_{A}}\Big)^{2m_{A}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{1}}\,}{E_{A}}\Big)^{\ell_{A}-\ell_{1A}-2m_{A}-p_{A}-4}.

(95)

Now we can regroup energy-ratio factors, and get:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{01(2)\cdots(N)(\overleftarrow{A})}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}2^{N+2}\cos(\pi p_{01\cdots NA}/2)\Gamma(\ell_{12\cdots NA}+2m_{12\cdots NA}+p_{012\cdots NA}+4N+5)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=2}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{i}}}\,}{\ell_{i}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{i}+p_{i}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{i}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{i}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{i}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+p_{i}+1}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{1}}}\,}{\ell_{1}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{1A}+2m_{A}+q_{1}+p_{1A}+4}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{1}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{1}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{A}+p_{1}+1}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{A}}}\,}{\ell_{A}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{-\ell_{1}-2m_{A}-p_{A}}\,}{2}$}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{A}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{A}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{A}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{A}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{A}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{1}+p_{A}+1}.

(96)

At this point, it is obvious that we should switch the summation variable
\ell_{1}\leftrightarrow\ell_{A}
to make the expression into a more organized form:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{01(2)\cdots(N)(\overleftarrow{A})}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}2^{N+2}\cos(\pi p_{01\cdots NA}/2)\Gamma(\ell_{12\cdots NA}+2m_{12\cdots NA}+p_{012\cdots NA}+4N+5)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=2}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{i}}}\,}{\ell_{i}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{i}+p_{i}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{i}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{i}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{i}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+p_{i}+1}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{1}}}\,}{\ell_{1}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{1A}+2m_{A}+q_{1}+p_{1A}+4}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{1}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{1}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{1}+p_{1}+1}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{A}}}\,}{\ell_{A}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{-\ell_{A}-2m_{A}-p_{A}}\,}{2}$}-\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{A}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{A}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{A}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{A}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{A}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{A}+p_{A}+1}.

(97)

The structure of the series is now quite clear: All energy ratios have
E_{0}
in the denominator, meaning that
E_{0}
still plays the role of the largest energy, even after we add the new wrong line. This series automatically gives an order
E_{A}<E_{0}
. At this point, it seems no further progress can be made, and the coefficients look quite messy. However, let us use the following identity to rewrite the Pochhammer factor in the last line:

\displaystyle(-a+b-m)_{m+1}(-a-b-m)_{m+1}=(a+b)_{m+1}(a-b)_{m+1}.~~~~(m\in\mathbb{Z})

(98)

Then we get:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{01(2\cdots N\overleftarrow{A})}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}2^{N+2}\cos(\pi p_{01\cdots NA}/2)\Gamma(\ell_{12\cdots NA}+2m_{12\cdots NA}+p_{012\cdots NA}+4N+5)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=2}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{i}}}\,}{\ell_{i}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{i}+p_{i}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{i}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{i}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{i}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+p_{i}+1}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{1}}}\,}{\ell_{1}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{1A}+2m_{A}+q_{1}+p_{1A}+4}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{1}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{1}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{1}+p_{1}+1}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{A}}}\,}{\ell_{A}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{A}+p_{A}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{A}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{A}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{A}}\,}{2E_{0}}\Big)^{2m_{A}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{A}}\,}{E_{0}}\Big)^{\ell_{A}+p_{A}+1}.

(99)

We see that the new terms generated by adding the wrong line are exactly the same as adding a correct line. Thus, so far as the CIS of the whole graph is concerned, we can flip the directions of all wrong lines to make the graph into a partially ordered tree graph, which is exactly the induced partial order. This result can also be understood directly from the series expression for a partially ordered tree (
93
), where the energy dependence takes the form of
K_{i}/E_{1}
and
E_{i}/E_{1}
for all
i\neq 1
. Thus, the convergence of this series only requires these ratios to be small, and it says nothing about the relative sizes of all
K_{i}
and
E_{i}
for
i\neq 1
. Thus, we have completed the construction of the CIS for a general tree graph with a general vertex energy ordering.

5.3
Summary: CISs as massive family trees

Let us summarize the main result of this section. We have used the inhomogeneous recursion formulae (
61
) and (
77
) to recursively construct the completely inhomogeneous solution (CIS). It turns out possible to find a general formula for the CIS of arbitrary massive tree graphs
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
with
V\geq 1
vertices.

As it appears, the result for the CIS is a multivariate hypergeometric function, and thus a useful way to express this function is a series expansion. To write down a series, we pick up the largest vertex energy, which we call
E_{1}
. Note that the choice of the largest vertex energy
E_{1}
is arbitrary: We can pick up any vertex energy to be the largest one, and call it
E_{1}
. Once this choice is made, the graph immediately acquires a partial order structure, called the induced order. That is, every line becomes directional, flowing from the
E_{1}
site outwards throughout all lines and vertices in the graph, as shown in the middle graph of Fig.
5
. This is a natural massive generalization of the family trees introduced in
[
80
,
68
]
for conformal scalar amplitudes. Thus, we call it a
massive family tree
.

Once we have a massive family tree structure for a graph of
V
vertices, we can express the CIS as a power series in
E_{i}/E_{1}
and
K_{i}/E_{1}
, with
i=2,\cdots,N
. Here, we use the convention that a line energy
K_{i}
shares the same subscript
i
with the vertex energy
E_{i}
it flows into. There are a total of
2(V-1)
variables, and correspondingly, there are
2(V-1)
fold summations. Thus, we can assign a summation variable
\ell_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
to
E_{i}/E_{1}
, and a summation variable
m_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
to
K_{i}/E_{1}
. As a result, with any Vertex
i\neq 1
, we associate a set of variables
(E_{i},K_{i},p_{i},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i},\ell_{i},m_{i})
.

With these preparations, we can write down the result for the CIS of
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
as the following series:

\displaystyle\text{CIS}\,\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V}\big]=

\displaystyle~\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}2^{V}\cos(\pi p_{1\cdots V}/2)\Gamma(q_{1}+p_{1}+1)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{i=2}^{V}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{i}}}\,}{\ell_{i}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\ell_{i}+q_{i}+p_{i}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{i}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{i}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{i}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+p_{i}+1}.

(100)

There is one more parameter
q_{i}
introduced in this expression, called the family parameter, which encodes the family tree structure of a graph. It is defined by:

q_{i}\equiv\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\ell\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}+2\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mum\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mup\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}+4N_{i},

(101)

where
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\ell\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}
means the sum of all
\ell_{j}
’s over the descendant vertices of Vertex
i
.
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mum\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}
and
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mup\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}
are defined similarly, and
N_{i}
is the number of descendant vertices of Vertex
i
. Thus, we see that there is a one-to-one correspondence between a general tree graph with its CIS: Given the graph, we can directly write down the CIS, and given the CIS, we can easily recover the graph by decoding the tree structure from the explicit forms of all
q_{i}
. Thus, from now on, we will use our notation for massive family trees to denote the CIS of a graph:

\text{CIS}\,\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V}\big]=\big\llbracket{\mathscr{P}(1\cdots V)}\big\rrbracket,

(102)

where the partial order
\mathscr{P}
is the induced order from the maximal vertex energy site which can be arbitrarily chosen.

From the viewpoint of bulk time integrals, the CIS corresponds to the part of the integral where all time variables are nested by
\theta
functions. In a bulk propagator
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}
, only the same-sign propagators
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\pm\pm}
contains
\theta
function (see (
5
)). Therefore, in the integral (
11
), the CIS is contributed only by terms with all SK indices being equal, namely
\mathsf{a}_{1}=\cdots=\mathsf{a}_{V}=\pm
. On the other hand, the
\theta
function has the freedom to change direction via
\theta(\tau_{i}-\tau_{j})=1-\theta(\tau_{j}-\tau_{i})
. So the property of being fully nested only specifies the nested integral up to additive factorized terms. We need further conditions to identify the CIS from the all-plus and all-minus time integrals, and the condition is that the CIS is analytic for each of
E_{i}\rightarrow 0
and
K_{i}\rightarrow 0
(
i=2,\cdots,V
). With this condition, a direct analysis of integral shows that the CIS is contributed by the part of the integral where all
\theta
-functions follow the
induced order
of the graph (the middle graph of Fig.
5
). Thus, we see that there is a correspondence between the energy flow and the time flow. This observation will be useful when we cut the CIS below.

The massive family tree (
100
) is a
2(V-1)
-variable hypergeometric function of Horn type
[
95
]
. As a series of
2(V-1)
-fold summations, the summand of (
100
), called
S(\{\ell,m\})
, is a function of
2(V-1)
summation variables
\ell_{i},m_{i}
(
i=2,\cdots,V
). Such a series gives rise to a hypergeometric function if it has a finite convergence region, and if the summand
S(\{\ell,m\})
satisfies the following property: For any
i=2,\cdots,V
and any
\ell_{i},m_{i}\geq 0
,
S(\ell_{i}+1)/S(\ell_{i})
and
S(m_{i}+1)/S(m_{i})
are both rational functions of all
\ell_{i}
and
m_{i}
. Clearly, we are not super well equipped to decode all properties of such hypergeometric functions. Therefore, it is important to develop different series representations. In this work, we have found series expanded in the inverse of
any
vertex energy. Thus, for every
V
-site massive family tree, we have
V
different inequivalent series expansions. Together with appropriate homogeneous parts to be discussed below, they give rise to analytical continuations of each other. This already covers many regions in the physically accessible parameter spaces. It is an interesting future problem to find more series expansions for the massive family trees.

We finish this section by making a more conjectural observation. As shown in
[
68
]
, any time integrals nested by a partially ordered structure can be further decomposed into a sum of standard
iterated integrals
in which the time order is a total order. (That is, every mother has only one daughter.) For iterated integrals, there is a notion of grading such that we can define a transcendental weight for this class of functions
[
96
]
. We conjecture that it is possible to assign transcendental weights to our series representations of CISs, which count the minimally required layers of summations with the condition that the summand has a hypergeometric form. (That is,
S(\ell_{i}+1)/S(\ell_{i})
being rational as mentioned above.) We conjecture that the CIS of a
V
-site graph has weight
2(V-1)
, and our series representation (
100
) is optimal in the sense of counting the transcendental weight, meaning that, in general, the layer of summation cannot be further reduced without spoiling the rational form of neighboring summand ratios.

For comparison, we can also get a series representation for an arbitrary massive tree graph with partial Mellin-Barnes representation plus the family tree decomposition as described in
[
80
]
. However, the series obtained in this way is not optimal, because the CIS of a
V
-site graph involves
3(V-1)
summations, among which
2(V-1)
comes from the PMB representation of Hankel functions, and
(V-1)
comes from the (conformal) family tree integrals.

As we will see below, the layers of summations do not change after we include the homogeneous solutions. That is, the “signals” share the same transcendental weight as the “background,” with the only difference of being factorized (similar to the relation between
\log^{2}(z)
and
\text{Li}_{2}(z)
). Thus, it seems that the transcendental weight can be directly defined with respect to a whole graph, not only to its CIS. However, if we go to degenerate configurations when some line and vertex energies are set equal, results from previous works
[
60
,
61
]
show that the weight of the function can decrease. It would be very interesting to better understand these phenomena and we leave it for a future work.

6  Building Homogenous Solutions from Cuts

In this section, we consider the (partially) homogeneous solutions to the differential equation system. Again, by a homogeneous solution, we mean part of the whole correlator that is annihilated by at least one of the
2V-2
differential operators. Given the CIS from the last section, it is easy to obtain homogeneous solutions by taking cuts of the CIS.

6.1
Single cut of the CIS

We first describe how to cut a single line in the CIS to get a solution homogeneous with respect to the cut line, but inhomogeneous to all other lines. Later, we generalize the result to multiple cuts.

Figure 10:
The single cut of a tree graph over the line
K
, which connects vertices
E_{i}
and
E_{j}
.
E_{1}
(
E_{2}
) is the largest vertex energy of the left (right) subgraph, which may or may not coincide with
E_{i}
(
E_{j}
). We take
E_{1}>E_{2}
.

We still work with a general graph
{\mathcal{G}}
of
V
vertices, as shown in Fig.
10
. We fix the order for all vertex energy
E_{1}>E_{i_{2}}>\cdots E_{i_{V}}
, and we work with the region where all line energies
K_{i}

(i=2,\cdots,V)
are smaller than all vertex energies. Then, we want to take one cut of the CIS for
{\mathcal{G}}
with respect to a particular line
K
, which connects two vertices
E_{i}
and
E_{j}
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
E_{i}
and the maximal energy
E_{1}
are on the same side with respect to the line
K
, which we call the “left” side, and
E_{j}
is on the other side, namely the “right” side. (In particular,
E_{i}
can be
E_{1}
.) Also, for convenience, let us define, for the argument of this subsection:

\displaystyle r_{i}\equiv K/E_{i},

\displaystyle r_{j}\equiv K/E_{j}.

(103)

Then, by a single cut of CIS, we mean the part of the full correlator that is annihilated only by
\mathcal{D}_{r_{i}}
and
\mathcal{D}_{r_{j}}
but not any other differential operators in our differential equation system
6
6

6

Similar to the CIS, this definition specifies the single cut only up to terms homogeneous with respect to other lines. This ambiguity is removed by imposing correct analytic behavior, to be detailed below.
.

It is easier to switch to the bulk time integral perspective at this point. Let us isolate the part of time integrals related to vertices
E_{i}
and
E_{j}
:

{\mathcal{G}}=\sum_{\mathsf{a}_{i},\mathsf{a}_{j}=\pm}\int{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{i}z_{i}}{\mathrm{d}}z_{j}\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{j}(-z_{j})^{p_{j}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}_{j}z_{j}}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}_{i}\mathsf{a}_{j}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}_{i}}^{\text{(L)}}\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}_{j}}^{\text{(R)}},

(104)

where
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}_{i}}^{\text{(L)}}
consists of time integrals over all left-side vertices except for the factors explicitly shown in (
104
), with the SK index
\mathsf{a}_{i}
not summed. Likewise,
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}_{j}}^{\text{(R)}}
collects time integrals over all right-side vertices that are not explicitly shown in (
104
).

It is important to note that, since we are executing only one cut, the left and right subgraphs are themselves fully nested. (That is, they are also CISs of the two subgraphs.) Therefore, all vertices in
\mathcal{I}_{\mathsf{a}_{i}}^{\text{(L)}}
share the same SK index as the
z_{i}
vertex. That is why we can use a single SK index
\mathsf{a}_{i}
(
\mathsf{a}_{j}
) for all left-side (right-side) time integrals.

The CIS we have found in (
100
) corresponds to a piece analytic in
1/E_{1}\rightarrow 0
(up to powers with exponent
p_{2\cdots V}
, which could be nonanalytic if the exponent is noninteger). From the bulk integral perspective, this corresponds to the contribution from terms in the inhomogeneous propagator
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\pm\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})
that place
\tau_{i}
earlier than
\tau_{j}
. Explicitly, we rewrite the inhomogeneous bulk propagator as:

\displaystyle\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\pm\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})=\Big[\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\pm\mp}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mp\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})\Big]\theta(r_{j}z_{j}-r_{i}z_{i})+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mp\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j}).

(105)

Then, the CIS is contributed by the first term on the right-hand side, and the second term, together with the contributions from the other SK branches (
\mathsf{a}_{i}=-\mathsf{a}_{j}
), gives rise to the cut part. As we shall see from the result of this section, the cut part is fully nonanalytic as
1/E_{1}\rightarrow 0
, and corresponds to the cosmological collider signals of both nonlocal and local types. This is well understood as a cutting rule for computing cosmological collider signals
[
97
,
79
,
78
]
.

It is important that the cut part in (
105
) is asymmetric with respect to the replacement
i\leftrightarrow j
. The asymmetry arises from the fact that we have given a particular order to all vertex energies. It is clear from the argument leading to (
105
) that the “direction” of the cut is determined by comparing the maximal vertex energies of the two subgraphs
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
(see Fig.
10
), rather than by comparing the two endpoint energies of the cut line (
E_{i}
and
E_{j}
).

It is now clear that our asymmetric cutting rule means that, if the largest vertex energy
E_{1}
is on the left subgraph, then we do the replacement:

\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\pm\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})\Rightarrow\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mp\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j}).

(106)

Then the integral is:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{}}{Cut}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}\big]=-\int{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}{\mathrm{d}}z_{j}(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}(-z_{j})^{p_{j}}\Big[e^{+\mathrm{i}(z_{i}+z_{j})}\mathcal{I}_{+}^{\text{(L)}}-e^{-\mathrm{i}(z_{i}-z_{j})}\mathcal{I}_{-}^{\text{(L)}}\Big]\mathcal{I}_{+}^{\text{(R)}}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{-+}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})+\text{c.c.}.

(107)

Now the
z_{i}
and
z_{j}
integrals are factorized, so we can compute them separately. This can be done by a direct late-time expansion of the homogeneous propagator
7
7

7

We emphasize that this expansion leads to a series expression for the final result of finite convergence region, and thus is
not
an approximation.
:

\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{-+}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})=\sigma(r_{i}z_{i})\sigma^{*}(r_{j}z_{j}),

(108)

where the mode function
\sigma(rz)
for the scalar with mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
can be written as:

\displaystyle\sigma(rz)=

\displaystyle~\sum_{\mathsf{c}=\pm}\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}A_{m,\mathsf{c}}(r)(-z)^{2m+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{c}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+3/2}

(109)

\displaystyle A_{m,\mathsf{c}}=

\displaystyle~\sqrt{\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{\pi}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{e^{+\pi\mathsf{c}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu/2}\Gamma(-m-\mathrm{i}\mathsf{c}_{1}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{m!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r}\,}{2}\Big)^{2m+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{c}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+3/2}.

(110)

Now, for later convenience, let us define the following integrals:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{\pm}^{\mathsf{c}_{1}}=

\displaystyle\pm\mathrm{i}\int{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}(-z_{i})^{p_{i}+2m_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{c}_{1}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+3/2}e^{\pm\mathrm{i}z_{i}}\mathcal{I}_{\pm}^{\text{(L)}},

(111)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}_{\pm}^{\mathsf{c}_{2}}=

\displaystyle\pm\mathrm{i}\int{\mathrm{d}}z_{j}(-z_{j})^{p_{j}+2m_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mathsf{c}_{2}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+3/2}e^{\pm\mathrm{i}z_{i}}\mathcal{I}_{\pm}^{\text{(R)}},

(112)

where
\mathsf{c}_{1,2}
are parameters labeling the shadow pair
\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
associated with the internal line
K
.
These are the time integrals of two subgraphs after the cuts, with the SK indices (subscripts) not summed. We also define the corresponding integrals with the SK indices summed:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathsf{c}}={\mathcal{L}}_{+}^{\mathsf{c}}+{\mathcal{L}}_{-}^{\mathsf{c}},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathsf{c}}={\mathcal{R}}_{+}^{\mathsf{c}}+{\mathcal{R}}_{-}^{\mathsf{c}}.

(113)

Then, after substituting the expansion (
108
) and (
109
) into the integral (
107
). it is easy to see that the single cut
\mathop{\text{}}{Cut}\limits_{K}[{\mathcal{G}}]
can be expressed as a sum of factorized terms as:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{}}{Cut}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}\big]=

\displaystyle\sum_{\{\mathsf{c},m\}}\Big[A_{m_{1},\mathsf{c}_{1}}(r_{i})A_{m_{2},\mathsf{c}_{2}}^{*}(r_{j}){\mathcal{L}}_{+}^{\mathsf{c}_{1}}{\mathcal{R}}_{+}^{-\mathsf{c}_{2}}+A_{m_{1},\mathsf{c}_{1}}^{*}(r_{i})A_{m_{2},\mathsf{c}_{2}}(r_{j}){\mathcal{L}}_{+}^{-\mathsf{c}_{1}}{\mathcal{R}}_{-}^{\mathsf{c}_{2}}\Big]+\text{c.c.},

(114)

However, the integrals
{\mathcal{L}}_{\pm}^{\mathsf{c}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}_{\pm}^{\mathsf{c}}
in the above expression are not yet in the form of CISs for the left and right subdiagrams, since they carry unsummed SK indices. It is desirable if we can combine them into real CISs with SK indices summed. This can be partially achieved by exploiting the explicit expressions of
A_{m,\mathsf{c}}
. After substituting (
110
) into (
114
) and with a little bit of algebra, we get:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{}}{Cut}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}\big]=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{\pi}\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{m_{1}!m_{2}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3/2}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2m_{2}+3/2}{\mathcal{L}}^{+}\bigg[\Gamma[-m_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-m_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu]\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}r_{j}}\,}{4}\Big)^{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{\mathcal{R}}^{+}

\displaystyle+\Gamma[-m_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-m_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu]\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{r_{j}}\Big)^{+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(e^{+\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{\mathcal{R}}_{+}^{-}+e^{-\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{\mathcal{R}}_{-}^{-})\bigg]+\text{c.c.}.

(115)

Most of the terms in this expression are already written in terms of the complete CISs
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathsf{c}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathsf{c}}
, except for the last term in the square bracket, where we have a pair of
{\mathcal{R}}_{\pm}^{-}
and they cannot be trivially combined into a single CIS
{\mathcal{R}}^{-}
. To solve this problem, we go back to the explicit expression of a general CIS discussed in the previous section. Recall that all massive family trees discussed before are constructed recursively from the 1-site graph
\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket
in (
81
), which is itself a bulk time integral with the SK index summed. Thus, to get a massive family tree with an unsummed SK index, we only need to work with the 1-site integral (
81
) without the summation over
\mathsf{a}=\pm
. Explicitly, let us denote the 1-site integral with unsummed SK index as
\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket_{\pm}
, then:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket_{\pm}=e^{\mp\mathrm{i}\pi p/2}\Gamma(1+p);

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket=\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket_{+}+\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket_{-}=2\cos(\pi p/2)\Gamma(1+p).

(116)

Now, we can choose to carry out the recursive construction of any tree graph based on either
\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket
or
\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket_{\pm}
. The net effect is that we change all
p
in (
116
) by the sum of
p
’s on all the vertices of the graph which we call
p_{\text{tot}}
. Apart from the cosine or phase factors, all other factors in the final CIS are actually ignorant of the SK index. From this analysis, we get the following result that holds for arbitrary massive family trees:

\displaystyle\displaystyle\frac{\,{\big\llbracket{1\cdots N}\big\rrbracket_{\pm}}\,}{\big\llbracket{1\cdots N}\big\rrbracket}=\displaystyle\frac{\,{e^{\mp\mathrm{i}\pi p_{\text{tot}}/2}}\,}{2\cos(\pi p_{\text{tot}}/2)}.

(117)

In particular, we have
{\mathcal{R}}_{\pm}^{-}/{\mathcal{R}}^{-}=e^{\mp\mathrm{i}\pi p_{\text{R}}/2}/[2\cos(\pi p_{\text{R}}/2)]
, where, again,
p_{\text{R}}
is total twist (namely, the sum of
p
’s on all vertices) of
{\mathcal{R}}^{-}

after
the shift
p_{j}\rightarrow p_{j}+2m_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+3/2
. (See (
112
).) Then, we can use this result to rewrite the single cut of the graph as:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{}}{Cut}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}\big]=

\displaystyle\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{\pi}\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{m_{1}!m_{2}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3/2}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{j}}\,}{2}\Big)^{2m_{2}+3/2}{\mathcal{L}}^{+}\Bigg\{\Gamma[-m_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-m_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu]\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}r_{j}}\,}{4}\Big)^{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{\mathcal{R}}^{+}

\displaystyle+\Gamma[-m_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu,-m_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu]\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{r_{i}}\,}{r_{j}}\Big)^{+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{\text{R}}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi p_{\text{R}}}\,}{2}$}\big)}{\mathcal{R}}^{-}\Bigg\}+\text{c.c.}

(118)

In this form, we have succeeded in writing the single cut of a graph in terms of two CISs of the subgraphs.

Summary and remarks

Let us summarize our result in a clearer form for easier generalization in the following. Suppose that we are considering a
V
-vertex graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
with
E_{1}
chosen as the largest energy variable, and we take the single cut of
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
with respect to a given line
K_{\alpha}
that connecting Vertices
i
and
j
. Further assuming that Vertex
i
is on the same side as the maximal-energy vertex
E_{1}
. Then, the single cut
\mathop{\text{}}{Cut}\limits_{K_{\alpha}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V}\big]
can be expressed as the sum of products of
augmented
and
flattened
CISs of two subdiagrams, as:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{}}{Cut}_{K_{\alpha}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{V}\big]=\big\llbracket{\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu1\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\cdots i^{\sharp}\cdots}\big\rrbracket\Big\{\big\llbracket{\cdots j^{\sharp}\cdots}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{\cdots j^{\flat}\cdots}\big\rrbracket\Big\}+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}).

(119)

Here we added a hat to
\mkern 2.0mu\widehat{\mkern-2.0mu1\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu
to highlight that
E_{1}
is chosen as the largest vertex energy in this expression. Also, we have defined the following
tuned
massive family trees with one entry augmented (
\sharp
) or flattened (
\flat
), as:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{\cdots i^{\sharp}\cdots}\big\rrbracket\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{\pi}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}\,}{m!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{2E_{i}}\Big)^{2m+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2}\big\llbracket{\cdots i\cdots}\big\rrbracket_{p_{i}\rightarrow p_{i}+2m+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2}~,

(120)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{\cdots i^{\flat}\cdots}\big\rrbracket\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{\pi}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}\,}{m!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{2E_{i}}\Big)^{2m-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2}

\displaystyle\times\bigg\{\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{\text{tot}}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi p_{\text{tot}}}\,}{2}$}\big)}\big\llbracket{\cdots i\cdots}\big\rrbracket\bigg\}_{p_{i}\rightarrow p_{i}+2m-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2}~,

(121)

where
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}
is the mass of Line
K_{\alpha}
,
p_{\text{tot}}
is the total twist of the subgraph
before
the shift
p_{i}\rightarrow p_{i}+2m-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2
. Moreover, we rewrite complex conjugation in previous derivations as
(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})
, assuming that
\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}
is the only complex variable that needs to flip sign under complex conjugation. In particular, we assume that both subgraphs before the tuning are real. The appearance of
(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})
is easy to understand: each bulk massive scalar gives rise to two late-time falloffs with conformal dimensions
\Delta_{\pm}=\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}
, which are called shadow conjugates of each other. We will borrow this terminology and call
(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})
the shadow conjugate.

Clearly, the cut is not symmetric with respect to the two subgraphs, due to our choice of energy orderings. We can think of the cut as being directional, flowing from the subgraph containing the largest vertex energy to the other subgraph, as shown in Fig.
10
. Then, the subgraph containing the largest vertex energy is augmented, and the other subgraph is both augmented and flattened. (Note that the augmentation and flattening of the same graph are not complex conjugates of each other.)

The two subgraphs are themselves CISs. Therefore, we can apply the result of (
100
) to directly write down their expression. In particular, the CIS of the
i
-subgraph is still expressed as a series expanded in
1/E_{1}
since it contains the maximal energy site
E_{1}
. The other subgraph that contains Vertex
j
, however, is independent of
E_{1}
. Therefore, we need to find the largest vertex energy within this subgraph, called
E_{2}
, and expand the CIS of
j
-subgraph in terms of
1/E_{2}
.

In fact, it is still true that the whole subgraph is expanded in the inverse of the locally largest vertex energy (say,
E_{1}
) even after being tuned (augmented/flattened). This is clearly true if the tuned vertex (
E_{i}
) coincides with the vertex carrying the locally maximal energy, as one can easily see from (
120
)-(
121
). On the other hand, if
i\neq 1
, then we need to go back to the explicit expression for the CIS in (
100
), in which the vertex energy
E_{i}
appears in the tuned CIS through the power factor
(E_{i}/E_{1})^{\ell_{i}+(p_{i}+2m\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2)+1}
. Combining it with the other
E_{i}
-dependent energy ratio factor
(K_{\alpha}/2E_{i})^{2m\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2}
in (
120
)-(
121
), we have:

\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{2E_{i}}\Big)^{2m\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+(p_{i}+2m\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2)+1}=\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{i}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{\ell_{i}+p_{i}+1}.

(122)

Therefore, everything in (
120
)-(
121
) is actually expressed as expansion in the inverse of the locally maximal vertex energy
E_{1}
(
8
8

8

By comparing (
122
) with the expression of the CIS in (
100
), one may want to interpret the augmentation/flattening of a graph as shifting the summation variable
m_{i}
instead of shifting
p_{i}
. This is indeed possible if
E_{i}
is not the locally maximal energy
E_{1}
. When
E_{i}
and
E_{1}
coincide, however, this interpretation does not work since we do not introduce a summation variable
m
to the largest-energy site. Thus we choose the interpretation of shifting
p_{i}
for maximal generality, at the expense of obscuring the fact that all energies in (
120
)-(
121
) are actually divided by the maximal energy
E_{1}
.
).

The physical meaning of the single cut is quite clear: it represents the part of the whole diagram that shows the nonanalytic energy dependences due to the on-shell resonance of the
K_{\alpha}
line, but not to any other lines. In particular, the first term in (
119
) involving two augmented subgraphs, together with its complex conjugate, gives rise to the so-called “nonlocal signal” in the context of CC physics. The complex power
K_{\alpha}^{\pm 2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}}
in the nonlocal signal shows that it is nonanalytic as the line energy
K_{\alpha}\rightarrow 0
. As such, it gives rise to a characteristic oscillatory behavior as we send
K_{\alpha}\rightarrow 0
but keep all other energies fixed
9
9

9

We note that the single cut does not give all the nonlocal signals with respect to
K_{\alpha}
. The full nonlocal signal in
K_{\alpha}
corresponds to the sum of either cutting or not cutting all other lines. (The
K_{\alpha}
line is always cut.)
. As is known, it can be viewed as from the two-point correlator of the conformal operator arising from a late-time expansion of the massive line
K_{\alpha}
. In particular, it is symmetric with respect to the two sides, being ignorant about the relative sizes of vertex energies. Therefore, the two subgraphs in this part also appear symmetrically and are both augmented.

On the other hand, the second term in (
119
) with both augmented and flattened subgraphs, together with the complex conjugate, contributes to the so-called “local signal.” The local signal can be thought of as from the two-point correlator of the late-time conformal operator with its shadow conjugate. As such, it is asymmetric with respect to the exchange of two subgraphs, and is sensitive to the relative sizes of vertex energies. The generation of nonlocal and local signals from cutting the diagram symmetrically or asymmetrically are also investigated in previous works
[
97
,
79
,
78
,
81
,
98
]
.

6.2
Multiple cuts

We can easily generalize the above discussion to multiple cuts of a diagram. Again, a
V
-vertex graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
with
C
cuts over Line
K_{\alpha_{1}},\cdots,K_{\alpha_{C}}
corresponds to the part of the solution that is annihilated by
2C
differential operators involving
K_{\alpha_{n}}

(n=1,\cdots,C)
, but not by any of the rest of
2V-2C-2
operators. Also, the cut is sensitive to energy ordering. So we still work with the region where all vertex energies are arbitrarily ordered, but every line energy is required to be smaller than its two adjacent vertex energies.

Repeating the derivation of the previous subsection, we see that, cutting an arbitrary number of lines in a diagram can be done in the following way:

1.

The removal of
C
cut lines renders the original graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{V}
to
C+1
disjoint subgraphs, which we call
