\mathcal{H}_{1},\cdots,\mathcal{H}_{C+1}
. Since the vertex energies have been given an order from the very beginning, each subgraph has its own maximal vertex energy, which we call
E_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,E_{C+1}^{\prime}
, respectively.

2.

Every cut line
K_{\alpha}
connects two subgraphs, say
\mathcal{H}_{i}
and
\mathcal{H}_{j}
. Then, we compare their own locally maximal energies
E_{i}^{\prime}
and
E_{j}^{\prime}
to determine the direction of the cut: If
E_{i}^{\prime}>E_{j}^{\prime}
, the cut is directed to
\mathcal{H}_{j}
, and otherwise to
\mathcal{H}_{i}
. That is, the cut always carries a direction flowing from the subgraph carrying the larger maximal vertex energy to the other one.

3.

The result of the
C
cuts is then expressed as the sum and products of the CISs of
C+1
subgraphs. Explicitly, every cut generates a pair of terms together with their shadow conjugates, in which the “outgoing” vertex is augmented in both terms, and the “ingoing” vertex is augmented and flattened in the two terms, respectively. Note that augmentation and flattening are defined with respect to a vertex
and
a cut line, so it is possible that a vertex is both augmented and flattened at the same time.

Following the same procedure of deriving the single cut, we can find a general expression for a subgraph augmented and flattened an arbitrary number of times. Suppose a fully nested subgraph (also a CIS) is augmented
A
times, acting on the vertices
E_{1},\cdots,E_{A}
(with twists
p_{1},\cdots,p_{A}
), and flattened
F
times, acting on the vertices
E_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,E_{F}^{\prime}
(with twists
p_{1}^{\prime},\cdots,p_{F}^{\prime}
). These
A+F
vertices do not have to be distinct. Further assume that the
A
augmentations,
\sharp_{1},\cdots,\sharp_{A}
, are associated with cut lines
K_{1},\cdots,K_{A}
with mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\cdots,\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{A}
, and the
F
flattenings,
\flat_{1},\cdots,\flat_{F}
, are associated with cut lines
K^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,K^{\prime}_{F}
with mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}_{1},\cdots,\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu^{\prime}_{F}
. Then, the
A
-fold augmented and
F
-fold flattened CIS is given by:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{(\cdots)^{\sharp_{1}\cdots\sharp_{A}\flat_{1}\cdots\flat_{F}}}\big\rrbracket=\sum_{\{\ell,m\}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{\pi}\Big)^{(A+F)/2}\bigg\{\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\pi(p_{\text{tot}}/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1\cdots F}^{\prime})\big]}\,}{\cos(\pi p_{\text{tot}}/2)}\big\llbracket{\cdots}\big\rrbracket\bigg\}_{\vbox{\Let@\restore@math@cr\default@tag\halign{\hfil$\m@th\scriptstyle#$&$\m@th\scriptstyle{}#$\hfil\cr p_{\alpha}&\rightarrow p_{\alpha}+2\ell_{\alpha}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2\\
p_{\beta}^{\prime}&\rightarrow p_{\beta}^{\prime}+2m_{\beta}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta}^{\prime}+3/2\crcr}}}

\displaystyle~\times\prod_{\alpha=1}^{A}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-\ell_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}\,}{\ell_{\alpha}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{2E_{\alpha}}\Big)^{2\ell_{\alpha}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2}\prod_{\beta=1}^{F}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m_{\beta}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta}^{\prime})}\,}{m_{\beta}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\beta}^{\prime}}\,}{2E_{\beta}^{\prime}}\Big)^{2m_{\beta}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\beta}^{\prime}+3/2}.

(125)

In a graph with multiple cuts, importantly, the direction of a cut generally does not agree with either the induced order or the imposed order: The cut direction is always determined by comparing the two locally maximal energies of the two subgraphs joined by the cut line. However, it is easy to observe the following two facts: First, the direction of the single cut always coincides with the induced order of the graph, as already shown in the previous subsection; Second, the direction of the maximal cut (namely, all lines being cut) coincides with the imposed order of the graph. Thus we see that, although the CIS of a graph is ignorant about the relative sizes of all but the largest vertex energies, the full expression of the correlator is sensitive to energy ordering through the cuts.

Example 1: Triple cuts

The rules for taking multiple cuts are best illustrated with examples. Here we present two examples. More examples will be given in Sec.
7
.

Figure 11:
Taking the triple cuts of a 4-vertex star graph. The vertex energies are ordered as
E_{1}>E_{4}>E_{2},E_{3}
in the left graph, and
E_{1},E_{2}>E_{4}>E_{3}
in the right graph.

In our first example, we take the maximal cut of a 4-site star graph, as shown in Fig.
11
. We take Vertex
i
to have vertex energy
E_{i}
and twist
p_{i}
(
i=1,\cdots,4
), and Line
\alpha
to have line energy
K_{\alpha}
and mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}
(
\alpha=1,2,3
). To start, we need to order the four vertex energies, and we choose two orderings. In the first ordering, we take
E_{1}>E_{4}>E_{2},E_{3}
(left graph of Fig.
11
). Then, the triple cuts can be expressed as:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{K_{1},K_{2},K_{3}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{4}\big]=

\displaystyle~\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{4^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{4^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)\Big(\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{2^{\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)\Big(\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3^{\flat_{3}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)+\text{shadows}.

(126)

Here the terms collected in “shadows” mean to include all independent shadow conjugates of the cut lines, namely,
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i}

(i=1,2,3)
. Explicitly, in the above example:

\displaystyle(\cdots)+\text{shadows}=\Big\{\big[(\cdots)+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})\big]+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})\Big\}+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}).

(127)

Also, we have augmented or flattened certain vertices multiple times. Following the general formula (
6.2
), it is straightforward to write down their expressions. For instance:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{4^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\sum_{m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}=0}^{\infty}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{\pi}\Big)^{3/2}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{3}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}\,}{m_{\alpha}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{2E_{4}}\Big)^{2m_{\alpha}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2}\bigg]

\displaystyle\times\big\llbracket{4}\big\rrbracket_{p_{4}\rightarrow p_{4}+2m_{123}+\mathrm{i}(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3})+9/2}\;,

(128)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{4^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\sum_{m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}=0}^{\infty}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{\pi}\Big)^{3/2}\prod_{\alpha=2}^{3}\bigg[\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}\,}{m_{\alpha}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{2E_{4}}\Big)^{2m_{\alpha}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}+3/2}\bigg]\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{m_{1}!}

\displaystyle\times\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{2}}\Big)^{2m_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}+3/2}\bigg\{\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi p_{4}+2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi p_{4}}\,}{2}$}\big)}\big\llbracket{4}\big\rrbracket\bigg\}_{p_{4}\rightarrow p_{4}+2m_{123}+\mathrm{i}(-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}+\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3})+9/2}\;,~\text{etc.}\,.

(129)

Clearly, both of the triply dressed family trees can be expressed in terms of trivariate Lauricella function
\text{F}_{\text{C}}
. The other singly dressed family trees in (
126
) can all be expressed as the standard Gauss’s hypergeometric function
{}_{2}\text{F}_{1}
. We will not spell them out explicitly.

Now consider a different ordering of vertex energies, namely
E_{1},E_{2}>E_{4}>E_{3}
, shown in the right graph of Fig.
11
. In this case, the triple cuts can be expressed as:

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{K_{1},K_{2},K_{3}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{4}\big]=

\displaystyle~\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{4^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{4^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{4^{\sharp_{1}\flat_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{4^{\flat_{1}\flat_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)\Big(\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3^{\flat_{3}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)

\displaystyle~+\text{shadows},

(130)

where the shadow conjugates are again given by (
127
). Thus we see that the expression for the cuts is crucially dependent on the choice of vertex energy ordering.

Example 2: inward-pointing 3-site graph

We use this example to explain a subtlety when taking cuts, as shown in Fig.
12
. Organizing a graph into a sum of the CIS and its cuts amounts to regrouping all the bulk propagators. The time-ordered part gives the CIS and the factorized parts give the cuts. Due to the energy orderings, both the CIS and cuts are directional. It may happen that the direction of a line changes after we cut another line. This leads to a change of directional cut, namely, how we separate out the factorized part from an inhomogeneous propagator as in (
105
). Below we elucidate this point with the 3-site chain and show that our prescription leads to correct answers.

Figure 12:
The cuts of a 3-site chain graph with energy ordering
E_{1}>E_{3}>E_{2}
. The blue/red lines are correct/wrong lines.

When extracting the CIS of this 3-site chain, we should follow the induced order to decide the time ordering of all internal lines. Taking all vertices to be in the
+
branch, this means that the two bulk propagators
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{++}
in the integrand should be written as: (See (
5
) and (
108
).)

\displaystyle\Big[\big(\sigma_{1}^{*}\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{*}\big)\theta_{21}+\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{*}\Big]\Big[\big(\sigma_{3}^{*}\sigma_{4}-\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}^{*}\big)\theta_{32}+\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}^{*}\Big].

(131)

Here we use the shorthand notation for the mode functions of the internal line:
\sigma_{1}=\sigma(r_{1}z_{1})
,
\sigma_{2}=\sigma(r_{2}z_{2})
,
\sigma_{3}=\sigma(r_{3}z_{2})
, and
\sigma_{4}=\sigma(r_{4}z_{3})
. Also,
\theta_{21}\equiv\theta(r_{2}z_{2}-r_{1}z_{1})
and
\theta_{32}\equiv\theta(r_{4}z_{3}-r_{3}z_{2})
.
Expanding the four terms above, we get:

\displaystyle\underbrace{\big(\sigma_{1}^{*}\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{*}\big)\theta_{21}\big(\sigma_{3}^{*}\sigma_{4}-\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}^{*}\big)\theta_{32}}_{\text{CIS}}+\underbrace{\big(\sigma_{1}^{*}\sigma_{2}-\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{*}\big)\theta_{21}\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}^{*}}_{\text{cutting $K_{2}$}}

\displaystyle+\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{*}\big(\sigma_{3}^{*}\sigma_{4}-\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}^{*}\big)\theta_{32}+\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{*}\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}^{*}.

(132)

Clearly, the first term gives the CIS, and the rest of the terms correspond to cuts, including two single cuts and one double cut. Naively, one would expect that the two terms with a single
\theta
factor correspond to the two single cuts, and the last term with no
\theta
function corresponds to the double cut. However, things are a little more complicated than that. Below let us look at those cuts more closely.

For the single cuts, let us first consider cutting the right line. In this case, the left subgraph is a 2-site graph whose “local induced order” agrees with the original induced order of the whole graph. Thus, it is straightforward to see that the single cut over the right line
K_{2}
is given by the second term of the above expansion.

However, when we look at the single cut over the left line
K_{1}
, we see a new phenomenon: The resulting right subgraph, which is again a 2-site chain, has a locally induced order that is opposite to the original induced order, since we have
E_{3}>E_{2}
. Consequently, according to our rule for taking cuts, the single cut over
K_{1}
does not agree with the third term in the above expansion, but is given by the following term:

\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{*}(\sigma_{3}\sigma_{4}^{*}-\sigma_{3}^{*}\sigma_{4})\theta_{23}.~~~~~(\text{cutting $K_{1}$})

(133)

This apparent mismatch is fixed by another mismatch from the double cut. According to our rule, in the double cut, we compare the maximal energies of all subgraphs to determine the directions of the cuts. In this case, it agrees with the imposed order. Accordingly, the double cut is given by:

\sigma_{1}\sigma_{2}^{*}\sigma_{3}^{*}\sigma_{4}.~~~~~(\text{cutting $K_{1}$ and $K_{2}$})

(134)

Then, obviously, the sum of (
133
) and (
134
) agree with the second line of (
132
).

Although we are showing it with a specific example, the phenomenon is general: When we take a cut of a diagram, the induced order for some subgraphs may change, which amounts to regrouping the inhomogeneous propagators. The upshot is that we should decide the direction of a cut by comparing the two locally maximal vertex energies of the two subgraphs connected by the cut line, as described before.

7  Complete Solutions and Selected Examples

Given all the inhomogeneous and homogeneous pieces constructed in the previous two sections, we are now ready to present the full solution to an arbitrary tree graph
{\mathcal{G}}
. In this section, we will first summarize the complete solution, and then provide a few explicit examples, including the 2-site and 3-site chains, and a 4-site star graph. In all examples, we have numerically checked that our results agree perfectly with direct numerical integrations, but are way faster to evaluate than doing bulk time integral, especially when the number of vertices is large.

7.1
Complete solutions

Given a (dimensionless) tree diagram
{\mathcal{G}}
with
V
vertices, carrying vertex energies
E_{1},\cdots,E_{V}
and twists
p_{1},\cdots,p_{V}
, and
I=V-1
internal lines, carrying line energies
K_{1},\cdots,K_{I}
and masses
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\cdots,\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{I}
, the analytical answer for its integral (
11
) is generally a sum of products of multivariate hypergeometric functions. Working in the signal region, i.e., the region where all line energies are small compared to all vertex energies, an analytical expression for
{\mathcal{G}}
can be expressed as the sum of all cuts of
{\mathcal{G}}
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}=\sum_{i\in 2^{\{K\}}}\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{i}\big[{\mathcal{G}}\big].

(135)

Here
2^{\{K\}}
is the power set of the line-energy set
\{K_{\alpha}\}
, namely, the set of all subsets of
\{K_{\alpha}\}
(including the empty set and
\{K_{\alpha}\}
itself). Thus, there are a total of
2^{I}
independent cuts. Among these
2^{I}
terms, the uncut piece is the completely inhomogeneous solution
\text{CIS}[{\mathcal{G}}]
, and is given by (
100
). The single cut over a line
K
is given by (
119
). The multiple cuts can be defined similarly. In particular, a term with
C
cuts can be expressed as the sum of products of dressed CISs of
C+1
subgraphs. The two types of dressing, called augmentation and flattening, are defined in (
120
) and (
121
). The rule for augmenting and/or flattening a CIS an arbitrary number of times is given in (
6.2
).

With the above rule, we can directly write down the solution to an arbitrary tree graph
G
without doing any real computation. On the other hand, the structure of an arbitrary tree graph is fully fixed by decoding the family parameter
q_{i}
in the CIS, and also by all the cuts. Therefore, from the expression, it is also straightforward to recover the tree graph, including its topology and all parameters. Thus, there is a one-to-one relationship between the tree graph and the solution. In this sense, we can say that our procedure provides a WYSIWYG (what-you-see-is-what-you-get) solution to arbitrary trees.

As mentioned in Sec.
3.3
, there is a direct relation between the cut/uncut of a line and the signal/background in CC physics. From our explicit solutions, the meaning of CC signals can be better understood. There are two distinct types of signals that we can identify from a cut.

First, the nonlocal signal, coming from the part of the cut where both subgraphs are augmented (together with shadow conjugates), corresponds to a nonanalytic power of
K_{\alpha}^{\pm 2\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}}
as
K_{\alpha}\rightarrow 0
where
K_{\alpha}
is the line energy of the cut line. Thus, a nonlocal signal is associated with a line
K_{\alpha}
, and is to be found by sending the line energy
K_{\alpha}\rightarrow 0
.

On the other hand, there is a local signal, coming from the part of a cut where one subgraph is flattened. By examining the expressions for the cuts, we see that the local signal corresponds to a nonanalytic power of vertex energy ratios, such as
(E_{1}/E_{2})^{\pm\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}}
, where
E_{1,2}
are
not
vertex energies of the two endpoints of the cut line
K_{\alpha}
, but the maximally vertex energies of the two subgraphs. Therefore, a local signal is associated with a vertex
E_{i}
, and is to be found by sending
E_{i}\rightarrow\infty
.

Given the simple rules of writing down the full solutions as in (
135
), computing arbitrary massive tree graphs becomes a trivial task. Below we apply these rules to write down answers for a few simple tree diagrams, including the 2-site chain, the 3-site chain, and the 4-site star. Among them, the result for the 2-site chain is well known, while the results for the 3-site and 4-site graphs are new.

7.2
Two-site chain

We first consider the simplest nontrivial example, namely a 2-site chain, which can be, but not necessarily, from a 4-point correlator
\langle\varphi_{\bm{k}_{1}}\varphi_{\bm{k}_{2}}\varphi_{\bm{k}_{3}}\varphi_{\bm{k}_{4}}\rangle^{\prime}
of the massless inflaton
\varphi
with single massive exchange
\sigma_{\bm{K}}
in the
s
-channel, namely,
\bm{K}=\bm{k}_{1}+\bm{k}_{2}
, shown in Fig.
13
. This is a well-studied case and its explicit analytical expression can be found in many works, e.g.,
[
54
,
58
]
. Using our notation, the dimensionless graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
for the 2-site chain is specified by the two vertex energies
E_{1}=k_{12}
and
E_{2}=k_{34}
, together with the line energy
K\equiv|\bm{K}|
.

Figure 13:
The 2-site chain graph and a corresponding 4-point inflaton correlator.

Without loss of generality, we work in the region where
E_{1}>E_{2}
. The other region with
E_{2}>E_{1}
can be obtained by trivially switching labels. Then, it is straightforward to write down the answer for
{\mathcal{G}}_{2}
following the rules presented above:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{2}=\text{CIS}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{\text{2}}\big]+\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{K}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{\text{2}}\big]=\big\llbracket{12}\big\rrbracket+\Big\{\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{2^{\flat}}\big\rrbracket\Big)+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\Big\}.

(136)

Here the CIS corresponds to the “background,” and is given by:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{12}\big\rrbracket=\

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell}4\cos(\pi p_{12}/2)\Gamma(p_{12}+\ell+2m+5)}\,}{\ell!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{2}+\ell}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{2}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{p_{2}+\ell+1}.

(137)

This series can be summed into a Kampé de Fériet function
[
54
]
, but we do not spell it out explicitly.

The signal, on the other hand, corresponds to the single cut. Given the expression for the 1-site graph
\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket
in (
81
), the tuned graphs can be found according to (
120
) and (
121
). In this particular example, the tuned graphs can be summed into known hypergeometric functions. It is convenient to introduce the following function:

\displaystyle~\mathbf{F}^{p_{1}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(E_{1},K)\equiv\sum_{m=0}^{\infty}\sqrt{\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{\pi}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\,}{m!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\big\llbracket{1}\big\rrbracket_{p_{1}\rightarrow p_{1}+2m+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}

\displaystyle=-\displaystyle\frac{\,{2^{3/2+p_{1}}\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu+3/2)}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\sin(\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\left[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{1}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$},\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{1}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{7}\,}{4}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}\,}{2}$}\\
1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu\end{matrix}\middle|\displaystyle\frac{\,{K^{2}}\,}{E_{1}^{2}}\right],

(138)

where
{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}
is the dressed Gauss’s hypergeometric function and is defined in (
195
), and we have defined this augmented graph into a new function
\mathbf{F}^{p}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
. Then, the 3 tuned 1-site graphs can be easily expressed as:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp}}\big\rrbracket=\mathbf{F}^{p_{1}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(E_{1},K),

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp}}\big\rrbracket=\mathbf{F}^{p_{2}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(E_{2},K),

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{2^{\flat}}\big\rrbracket=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{2}$}(p_{2}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi}\,}{2}$}(p_{2}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{3}\,}{2}$}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)\big]}\mathbf{F}^{p_{2}}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}(E_{2},K).

(139)

Now, it is straightforward to check that our result (
136
) agrees with known results in the literature.

7.3
Three-site chain

Next, we consider the 3-site chain with two massive exchanges. This example has been computed analytically in
[
80
]
with PMB and FTD for a specific energy order. Related to this, a degenerate case with two massive exchanges was computed in
[
61
]
. Here we present the full result for general nondegenerate kinematics in the signal region, including all possible vertex energy orderings. A 3-site chain is specified by three vertex energies
E_{1},E_{2},E_{3}
, and two line energies
K_{1},K_{2}
. It can be from a 5-point or 6-point inflaton correlator with two massive exchanges, as shown in Fig.
14
.

Figure 14:
The 3-site chain graph and corresponding 6-point or 5-point inflaton correlators.

According to our rule, the answer for the 3-site chain is:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{3}=\text{CIS}\,\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{3}\big]+\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{K_{1}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{3}\big]+\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{K_{2}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{3}\big]+\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{K_{1},K_{2}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{3}\big].

(140)

However, to write down the explicit expressions for each term, we need to specify the energy order. There are three independent choices, 1)
E_{2}>E_{1},E_{3}
; 2)
E_{1}>E_{2}>E_{3}
; 3)
E_{1}>E_{3}>E_{2}
. Below we consider these cases in turn.

Case 1:
\bm{E_{2}>E_{1},E_{3}}

Using our terminology for massive family trees, both lines in this case are correct lines, so the graph is already a family tree by itself, and
E_{2}
is the mother of
E_{1}
and
E_{3}
. Thus, the general expression (
140
) can be unpacked in the following way:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{3}=

\displaystyle~\big\llbracket{2(1)(3)}\big\rrbracket+\Big\{\Big(\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{1^{\flat_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}}3}\big\rrbracket+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})\Big\}+\Big\{\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{2}}1}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3^{\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})\Big\}

\displaystyle+\Big\{\Big[\Big(\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{1^{\flat_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3^{\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})\Big]+(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}\rightarrow-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3})\Big\}.

(141)

Using (
100
), we have the following expression for the CIS:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{2(1)(3)}\big\rrbracket=\

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},m_{1},m_{2}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{8\cos\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi p_{123}}\,}{2}$}\big)(-1)^{\ell_{12}}\Gamma(p_{123}+\ell_{12}+2m_{12}+9)}\,}{\ell_{1}!\ell_{2}!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{1}+\ell_{1}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{1}+1}\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{3}+\ell_{2}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\times\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{2}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{1}}\,}{E_{2}}\Big)^{p_{1}+\ell_{1}+1}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{2E_{2}}\Big)^{2m_{2}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{3}}\,}{E_{2}}\Big)^{p_{3}+\ell_{2}+1}.

(142)

Then we have a collection of tuned CISs. First, the singly tuned 1-site CISs can be expressed in terms of the
\mathbf{F}^{p}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu}
function defined in (
138
), as:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket=\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}^{p_{1}}(E_{1},K_{1}),

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1^{\flat_{1}}}\big\rrbracket=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{1}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{1}+3/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\mathbf{F}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}^{p_{1}}(E_{1},K_{1}),

(143)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=\mathbf{F}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}^{p_{3}}(E_{3},K_{2}),

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{3^{\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{3}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{3}+3/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\mathbf{F}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}^{p_{3}}(E_{3},K_{2}).

(144)

Second, the doubly augmented 1-site CIS
\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket
can be expressed in terms of an Appell function:

\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=\mathbf{G}^{p_{2}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}(E_{2},K_{1},K_{2}),

(145)

where we define a new function
\mathbf{G}
for later convenience:

\displaystyle\mathbf{G}^{p_{2}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}(E_{2},K_{1},K_{2})\equiv\sum_{m_{1},m_{2}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{2}\,}{\pi}\bigg[\prod_{\alpha=1}^{2}\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma[-m_{\alpha}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}]}\,}{m_{\alpha}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{2E_{2}}\Big)^{2m_{\alpha}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}}\bigg]\big\llbracket{2}\big\rrbracket_{p_{2}\rightarrow p_{2}+2m_{12}+3+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12}}

\displaystyle=\displaystyle\frac{\,{2^{p_{2}+2}\sqrt{\pi}\sin\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\sin(\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})\sin(\pi\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{E_{2}}\Big)^{3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{E_{2}}\Big)^{3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\mathcal{F}_{4}\left[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{2}+4+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12}}\,}{2}$},\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{2}+5+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12}}\,}{2}$}\\
1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},1+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}\end{matrix}\middle|\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}^{2}}\,}{E_{2}^{2}},\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}^{2}}\,}{E_{2}^{2}}\right].

(146)

Here
\mathcal{F}_{4}
denotes the dressed Appell
\text{F}_{4}
function, whose definition can be found in (
196
).

Finally, there are two singly augmented 2-site CISs. Among them,
\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}}3}\big\rrbracket
can be expressed as:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}}3}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m_{1},m_{2}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell+m_{1}}4\sqrt{2/\pi}\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{23}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{2}$}\big]\Gamma(p_{23}+\ell+2m_{12}+{13}/{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{\ell!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{3}+\ell}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{m_{1}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{2}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{2E_{2}}\Big)^{2m_{2}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{3}}\,}{E_{2}}\Big)^{p_{3}+\ell+1},

(147)

and
\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{2}}1}\big\rrbracket
is easily obtained from
\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}}3}\big\rrbracket
by the replacement
(E_{3},p_{3})\rightarrow(E_{1},p_{1})
and
(K_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})\leftrightarrow(K_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})
.

Case 2:
\bm{E_{1}>E_{2}>E_{3}}

The 3-site graph with this energy order is also a massive family tree automatically, with three generations being
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
, and
E_{3}
. Thus, we can unpack the general expression (
140
) as:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{3}=

\displaystyle~\big\llbracket{123}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}}3}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{2^{\flat_{1}}3}\big\rrbracket\Big)+\big\llbracket{12^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3^{\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)

\displaystyle+\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{2^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)\Big(\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3^{\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)+\text{shadows}.

(148)

From now on we will not spell out the shadow conjugates. The CIS is given by:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{123}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{1},\ell_{2},m_{1},m_{2}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{8\cos\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi p_{123}}\,}{2}$}\big)(-1)^{\ell_{12}}\Gamma(p_{123}+\ell_{12}+2m_{12}+9)}\,}{\ell_{1}!\ell_{2}!\big(\frac{p_{23}+\ell_{12}+2m_{2}}{2}+\frac{13}{4}\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}{2}\big)_{m_{1}+1}\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{3}+\ell_{2}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\times\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{2}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{p_{2}+\ell_{1}+1}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{2}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{3}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{p_{3}+\ell_{2}+1}.

(149)

For the tuned CISs, the singly and doubly tuned 1-site CISs are the same as (
143
), (
144
), and (
145
). The new one is:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{2^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{2}+3+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{2}+3-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\mathbf{G}^{p_{2}}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}(E_{2},K_{1},K_{2}),

(150)

where
\mathbf{G}
is defined in (
146
).

Finally, there are three singly tuned 2-site CISs. Among them,
\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}}3}\big\rrbracket
has been given in (
147
), and the two new ones are:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{12^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m_{1},m_{2}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell+m_{1}}4\sqrt{2/\pi}\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{12}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})}\,}{2}$}\big]\Gamma(p_{12}+\ell+2m_{12}+{13}/{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})}\,}{\ell!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{2}+\ell+2m_{1}}\,}{2}$}+2+\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\,}{2}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})}\,}{m_{1}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{2}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{2}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{p_{2}+\ell+1},

(151)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{2^{\flat_{1}}3}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m_{1},m_{2}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell+m_{1}}4\sqrt{2/\pi}\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{23}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{2}$}\big]\Gamma(p_{23}+\ell+2m_{12}+{13}/{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{\ell!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{3}+\ell}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{m_{1}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{2}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{2E_{2}}\Big)^{2m_{2}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{3}}\,}{E_{2}}\Big)^{p_{3}+\ell+1}.

(152)

Case 3:
\bm{E_{1}>E_{3}>E_{2}}

This is an interesting case, as the energy order does not fit directly into a family tree structure, as already discussed at the end of Sec.
6
. According to this discussion, we should express (
140
) as:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{3}=

\displaystyle~\big\llbracket{123}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{32^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{32^{\flat_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)+\big\llbracket{12^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3^{\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)

\displaystyle+\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{2^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{2^{\flat_{1}\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\text{shadows}.

(153)

The CIS
\big\llbracket{123}\big\rrbracket
is identical to Case 2, as given in (
149
). The singly tuned and doubly tuned 1-site CISs are the same as (
143
), (
144
), (
145
), and (
150
). The new doubly tuned 1-site CISs are:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{1}\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{2}+3+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{2}+3+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\mathbf{G}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}^{p_{2}}(E_{2},K_{1},K_{2}),

(154)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{2^{\flat_{1}\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{2}+3+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{2}+3-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\mathbf{G}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}^{p_{2}}(E_{2},K_{1},K_{2}).

(155)

There are three singly tuned 2-site CISs, among which
\big\llbracket{12^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket
has been given in (
151
), and the two new ones are:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{32^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m_{1},m_{2}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell+m_{1}}4\sqrt{2/\pi}\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{23}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{2}$}\big]\Gamma(p_{23}+\ell+2m_{12}+13/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{\ell!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{2}+\ell+2m_{1}}\,}{2}$}+2+\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\,}{2}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{m_{1}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{3}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{2E_{3}}\Big)^{2m_{2}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{2}}\,}{E_{3}}\Big)^{p_{2}+\ell+1},

(156)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{32^{\flat_{1}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell,m_{1},m_{2}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell+m_{1}}4\sqrt{2/\pi}\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{23}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{2}$}\big]\Gamma(p_{23}+\ell+2m_{12}+13/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{\ell!\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{2}+\ell+2m_{1}}\,}{2}$}+2-\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\,}{2}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{m_{1}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{3}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{2E_{3}}\Big)^{2m_{2}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{2}}\,}{E_{3}}\Big)^{p_{2}+\ell+1}.

(157)

At this point, we have completed the presentation of full analytical results for the 3-chain graph in three different kinematic regions. We note again that the three seemingly different expressions, (
141
), (
148
), and (
153
), are merely different series expansions of the same object in (
140
). Clearly, we can think of them as analytical continuations of each other beyond their own convergence domain.

7.4
Four-site star

As the final example, we consider a 4-star graph with triple massive exchanges. Without trivially repeating the standard construction, let us consider a special case where the central vertex is not connected to any external lines, which corresponds to a 6-point inflaton correlator, as shown in Fig.
15
. We use this example to demonstrate how to take the limit of a vanishing vertex energy, which is
E_{4}\rightarrow 0
in this case. Note that this case is somewhat beyond the signal region we originally defined, which requires all vertex energies larger than all line energies. Here, one vertex energy,
E_{4}
, is smaller than all line energies. We show that only a slight change of our general rule is needed to get results in this case.

Figure 15:
The 4-site star graph and a corresponding 6-point inflaton correlator.

Given
E_{4}\rightarrow 0
, our original definition of the dimensionless graph (
8
) becomes singular for generic
p_{4}
. Thus, we slightly change the dimensionless graph in this case to be:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}=\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{4}}\Big)^{p_{4}+1}{\mathcal{G}}_{4},

(158)

where
{\mathcal{G}}_{4}
is the original dimensionless graph constructed from (
8
), and a factor of 2 is included for the notational simplicity of final expressions. Then, we can decompose
{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}
as usual:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}=

\displaystyle~\text{CIS}\,\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}\big]+{\sum_{\alpha=1}^{3}\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{K_{\alpha}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}\big]}+{\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta}\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{K_{\alpha},K_{\beta}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}\big]}+\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{K_{1},K_{2},K_{3}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}\big].

(159)

To write down explicit expressions, we need to pick up a particular energy order. Here we choose
E_{1}>E_{2}>E_{3}
. All other cases can be obtained by trivial permutations. Then, we have:

\displaystyle\text{CIS}\,\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}\big]=

\displaystyle~\big\llbracket{1\not{4}(2)(3)}\big\rrbracket,

(160)

\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha=1}^{3}\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{K_{\alpha}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}\big]}=

\displaystyle~\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{2\not{4}^{\sharp_{1}}3}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{2\not{4}^{\flat_{1}}3}\big\rrbracket\Big)+\big\llbracket{1\not{4}^{\sharp_{2}}3}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{2^{\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)

\displaystyle~+\big\llbracket{1\not{4}^{\sharp_{3}}2}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3^{\flat_{3}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)+\text{shadows},

(161)

\displaystyle{\sum_{\alpha\neq\beta}\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{K_{\alpha},K_{\beta}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}\big]}=

\displaystyle~\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{3\not{4}^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3\not{4}^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3\not{4}^{\sharp_{1}\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3\not{4}^{\flat_{1}\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)

\displaystyle+\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{2\not{4}^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{2\not{4}^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)\Big(\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3^{\flat_{3}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)

\displaystyle+\big\llbracket{1\not{4}^{\sharp_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{2^{\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)\Big(\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{3^{\flat_{3}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)+\text{shadows},

(162)

\displaystyle\mathop{\text{Cut}}_{K_{1},K_{2},K_{3}}\big[{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}\big]=

\displaystyle~\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket\Big(\big\llbracket{\not{4}^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{\not{4}^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{\not{4}^{\sharp_{1}\flat_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{\not{4}^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}\flat_{3}}}\big\rrbracket

\displaystyle+\big\llbracket{\not{4}^{\flat_{1}\flat_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{\not{4}^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{2}\flat_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{\not{4}^{\sharp_{1}\flat_{2}\flat_{3}}}\big\rrbracket+\big\llbracket{\not{4}^{\flat_{1}\flat_{2}\flat_{3}}}\big\rrbracket\Big)+\text{shadows}.

(163)

In these expressions, we have slashed the entry
\not{4}
to highlight that
E_{4}\rightarrow 0
but
p_{4}
is kept finite.

Now it is straightforward to find various CISs and tuned CISs. The CIS for the full graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}
is:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1\not{4}(2)(3)}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{2},\ell_{3},m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}=0}^{\infty}\frac{16\cos(\frac{\pi p_{1234}}{2})\Gamma(p_{1234}+\ell_{23}+2m_{123}+13)}{\big(\frac{p_{234}+\ell_{23}+2m_{23}}{2}+\frac{21}{4}\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}{2}\big)_{m_{1}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+p_{4}+4}

\displaystyle\times\prod_{\alpha=2}^{3}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(-1)^{\ell_{\alpha}}}\,}{\ell_{\alpha}!\big(\frac{p_{\alpha}+\ell_{\alpha}}{2}+\frac{5}{4}\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}}{2}\big)_{m_{\alpha}+1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{\alpha}}\,}{2E_{1}}\Big)^{2m_{\alpha}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{\alpha}}\,}{E_{1}}\Big)^{p_{\alpha}+\ell_{\alpha}+1}.

(164)

Here we have one less summation than an ordinary 4-site family tree
{\mathcal{G}}_{4}
, where we would have a series of the form
(E_{4}/E_{1})^{\ell_{1}+p_{4}+1}
. In
{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}
, the overall factor
(E_{4}/E_{1})^{p_{4}+1}
is replaced by
(K_{1}/2E_{1})^{p_{4}+1}
, and the series
(E_{4}/E_{1})^{\ell_{1}}
vanishes for all
\ell_{1}>0
.

Next, we list the tuned CISs. First, there are 5 singly tuned 1-site CISs. Among them,
\big\llbracket{1^{\sharp_{1}}}\big\rrbracket
is given in (
143
). The other four are:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{2^{\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=\mathbf{F}^{p_{2}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}(E_{2},K_{2}),

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{2^{\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{2}+{3}/{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{2}+{3}/{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\mathbf{F}^{p_{2}}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}(E_{2},K_{2}),

(165)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{3^{\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket=\mathbf{F}^{p_{3}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}(E_{3},K_{3}),

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{3^{\flat_{3}}}\big\rrbracket=\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{3}+{3}/{2}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{3}+{3}/{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\mathbf{F}^{p_{3}}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}(E_{3},K_{3}).

(166)

Second, there are 7 doubly tuned 2-site CISs, which can be conveniently expressed in terms of the following function:

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}^{p_{3},p_{4}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}(E_{3},K_{1},K_{2},K_{3})=\sum_{m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{8(-1)^{m_{23}}\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{34}+3+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12})}\,}{2}$}\big]\Gamma[p_{34}+2m_{123}+8+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12}]}\,}{\pi\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{4}+2m_{23}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{11}\,}{4}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12}}\,}{2}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{1}+1}}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma[-m_{2}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},-m_{3}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}]}\,}{m_{2}!m_{3}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{3}}\Big)^{2m_{2}+p_{4}+5/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{2E_{3}}\Big)^{2m_{3}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{3}}\,}{2E_{3}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3}.

(167)

Then:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{34^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\mathbf{H}^{p_{3},p_{4}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}(E_{3},K_{1},K_{2},K_{3}),

(168)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{34^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{34}+3+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{34}+3-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\mathbf{H}^{p_{3},p_{4}}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}(E_{3},K_{1},K_{2},K_{3}),

(169)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{34^{\sharp_{1}\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{34}+3+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{34}+3+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\mathbf{H}^{p_{3},p_{4}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}(E_{3},K_{1},K_{2},K_{3}),

(170)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{34^{\flat_{1}\flat_{2}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{34}+3-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{34}+3-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{12})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\mathbf{H}^{p_{3},p_{4}}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}(E_{3},K_{1},K_{2},K_{3}),

(171)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{24^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\mathbf{H}^{p_{2},p_{4}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}(E_{2},K_{1},K_{3},K_{2}),

(172)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{24^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{24}+3+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{13})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{24}+3-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\mathbf{H}^{p_{2},p_{4}}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}(E_{2},K_{1},K_{3},K_{2}),

(173)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{14^{\sharp_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{K_{2}}\Big)^{p_{4}+1}\mathbf{H}^{p_{1},p_{4}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}(E_{1},K_{2},K_{3},K_{1}).

(174)

Third, there are 4 singly tuned 3-site CISs, which can be conveniently expressed in terms of the following
\mathbf{I}
function:

\displaystyle~\mathbf{I}^{p_{2},p_{4},p_{3}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}(E_{2},E_{3},K_{1},K_{2},K_{3})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell_{2},m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{8\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{234}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{2}$}\big](-1)^{\ell_{2}+m_{3}}\Gamma(p_{234}+\ell_{2}+2m_{123}+21/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{\sqrt{\pi/2}\,\ell_{2}!\big(\frac{p_{34}+\ell_{2}+2m_{23}}{2}+4+\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\,}{2}$}\pm\frac{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}{2}\big)_{m_{1}+1}\big(\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{3}+\ell_{2}}\,}{2}$}+\mbox{$\frac{\,{5}\,}{4}$}\pm\mbox{$\frac{\,{\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}\,}{2}$}\big)_{m_{2}+1}}

\displaystyle\times\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(-m_{3}-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{m_{3}!}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{1}}\,}{2E_{2}}\Big)^{p_{4}+2m_{3}+5/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{2E_{2}}\Big)^{2m_{1}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{3}}\,}{2E_{2}}\Big)^{2m_{2}+3}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{E_{3}}\,}{E_{2}}\Big)^{p_{3}+\ell_{2}+1}.

(175)

Then, we have:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{24^{\sharp_{1}}3}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\mathbf{I}^{p_{2},p_{4},p_{3}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}(E_{2},E_{3},K_{1},K_{2},K_{3})

(176)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{24^{\flat_{1}}3}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{234}+3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\cos\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{234}+3/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\mathbf{I}^{p_{2},p_{4},p_{3}}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}(E_{2},E_{3},K_{1},K_{2},K_{3}),

(177)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{14^{\sharp_{3}}2}\big\rrbracket=

(179)

Finally, there are apparently 8 triply tuned 1-site CISs in (
163
), but with the main entry slashed. We cannot apply the general formula for the augmentation or flattening in this case since we cannot expand the expression in small
1/E_{4}
. However, the 1-site graph is simple enough and is nothing but the completely homogeneous solution to our differential equations, as discussed in (
3.4
). From (
47
), we see that the
N
-tuned 1-site graph is nothing but a Lauricella function. In our case, when setting
E_{4}\rightarrow 0
, the time integral (
45
) yields a
\delta
-function for Mellin variables. Then, completing the Mellin integral, it is straightforward to see that the triply tuned graphs are all expressible in terms of Appell functions, very similar to the well-known triple-
K
integral in the context of momentum-space CFT. Then, in the region of
E_{1}>E_{2},E_{3}
, we have:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{4^{\sharp_{1}\sharp_{2}\sharp_{3}}}\big\rrbracket=0,

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{4^{\flat_{1}\sharp_{2}\flat_{3}}}\big\rrbracket=2\sin(\mathrm{i}\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})\mathbf{J}^{p_{4}}_{-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}},

(183)

where
\mathbf{J}^{p_{4}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}
is a triple-

integral and can be expressed in terms of an Appell function:

\displaystyle\mathbf{J}^{p_{4}}_{\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}=

\displaystyle~\displaystyle\frac{\,{\sqrt{2\pi}\sin\big[\mbox{$\frac{\,{\pi(p_{4}-1/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{23})}\,}{2}$}\big]}\,}{\sin(\mathrm{i}\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1})\sin(\mathrm{i}\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2})\sin(\mathrm{i}\pi\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3})}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{2}}\,}{K_{1}}\Big)^{3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{2}}\Big(\displaystyle\frac{\,{K_{3}}\,}{K_{1}}\Big)^{3/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{3}}

\displaystyle\times\mathcal{F}_{4}\bigg[\begin{matrix}\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{4}+11/2+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{123}}\,}{2}$},\mbox{$\frac{\,{p_{4}+11/2-\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{1}+\mathrm{i}\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{23}}\,}{2}$}\\

(184)

This completes the presentation of the 4-site star graph
{\mathcal{G}}_{4}^{\prime}
.

8  Discussions and Outlooks

The program of cosmological collider (CC) physics has been actively investigated in recent years, with many interesting models found and many signal targets identified for future cosmological observations. Confronting CC models with real data calls for efficient and accurate computation of inflationary correlators. Meanwhile, cosmological correlators possess unique analytical structure which encodes rich information about QFTs in cosmological backgrounds, which deserve more systematic studies. Clearly, the analytical approach to cosmological correlators plays an essential role in both aspects.

The studies of cosmological correlators have been progressing fast in recent years. Many analytical calculations that were considered too difficult to be done a few years ago have now been made possible. Currently, the analytical computation of massive inflationary correlators at the tree level is largely a solved problem, and complete or partial results on massive loop correlators were obtained as well.

In this work, we carried out a systematic study of tree-level inflationary correlators with an arbitrary number of massive exchanges. We found a complete system of differential equations for these correlators and solved them in terms of multivariate hypergeometric functions. There is a natural structure emerged from this solution, with the completely inhomogeneous solution (CIS) expressed as a massive family tree, and the homogeneous solutions as the cuts of the CIS. With these results, we can now directly write down complete analytical results for arbitrary massive tree correlators in generic kinematics without doing any real computations, much like what we can do for tree amplitudes in flat spacetime. Also, compared with results from PMB and FTD, which are the only known previous methods that can analytically compute arbitrary massive trees, our results here have a more economic summation structure.

In earlier studies, the computation of nested bulk time integrals was often cited as a difficulty. Now, with family tree and massive family tree decompositions, the bulk time integrals are no longer obstacles. We choose to work with bulk time integrals directly, which can be important and useful for several reasons. First, the inflaton is known to be weakly coupled (small non-Gaussianity), and typical CC models with massive fields are perturbative in the bulk. Thus, it is more natural to work directly in the bulk. Second, for phenomenological studies of CC physics, most particle models with beyond-standard-model new physics have been built in the bulk spacetime, and it is important to find maps between bulk fields/interactions in these models and the signal shapes in inflationary correlators. Third, in realistic CC models, dS boosts and even the dilation are often broken. Thus it is important to develop techniques that do not rely on these symmetries. In these cases, it is often more convenient to work directly with bulk time integrals than pursuing a boundary correspondence.

On the other hand, from the explicit results obtained in this work, we can understand why the tree-level amplitudes are necessarily much more complicated than their flat-space counterparts. These inflationary correlators involve multi-layer nested time integrals of Hankel functions, which are often multivariate hypergeometric functions of high transcendental weights. Having high weights means that these functions can in no way be reduced to simpler and more familiar functions for generic parameters. This is in contrast to other amplitude stories such as the Parke-Taylor formula
[
99
]
, where the intermediate steps can be lengthy but the final result is simple. Therefore, whatever method we choose, we have to deal with those hypergeometric functions so long as we take the 3-momentum representation.

The hypergeometric functions/series identified in this work can be covered by known function classes such as Horn type
[
95
]
or GKZ type
[
100
]
, but our functions are a very special subclass: They are all massive family trees, with possible augmentation and flattening, and are fully determined by the twists and masses. Thus, it would be useful to develop specialized methods and techniques to understand this particular subclass of multivariate hypergeometric functions. In this work, we have made the first step, identified these functions and achieved expanding these functions in the signal regions, namely, when all vertex energies are larger than the line energies. It would be very useful to develop more expansion schemes, ideally around all possible singular regions. This type of expansions will be very useful for us to understand the analytical properties, including the singularity structures and functional relations, of these hypergeometric functions.

Our work has made a step towards more systematic studies of general massive inflation correlators. With the new results obtained here, many directions are now open for further explorations. We end this work by listing a few of them.

First, we have only considered generic kinematics in this work, meaning that all kinematic variables are not constrained. However, in realistic models, it also happens that some kinematic variables are constrained, which we call degenerate configurations. For instance, in the case of a two-point mixing between a massless external line and a massive internal line, we have a constraint
E=K
relating the corresponding vertex and line energies. It would be very interesting to push our results to various types of degenerate configurations.

Second, it was known that the differential equation for single exchange can be easily derived for fields with more complicated dispersion relations, such as non-unit sound speed, the helical chemical potential, or even when there is nontrivial scale dependence beyond pure dS
[
58
,
57
,
56
,
60
,
59
]
. For general graphs beyond the single exchange, can we find the complete differential equation systems and even solve them for these cases? We plan to address these questions in future works.

Third, it is known that the differential equations for tree correlators can be easily generalized to loop integrands in the case of conformal scalar amplitudes
[
69
,
74
]
. The massive amplitudes should be similar. Can we use these differential equations to better understand the analytical structure of loop graphs?

Finally but importantly, the differential equation system presented here could be a useful starting point for developing new numerical strategies for computing and studying massive inflationary correlators. Can we use these differential equations to find more efficient methods for computing these graphs, especially when the vertex number is large? We leave all these interesting questions for future studies.

A.2
Notations for massive family trees

In the main text, we use a notation
\big\llbracket{\cdots}\big\rrbracket
to represent general massive family trees, which correspond to CISs of general massive tree graphs. The use of this notation is in parallel with the family tree notation
\big[\cdots\big]
introduced in
[
68
]
, but also with some differences. Here we briefly introduce this notation.

Similar to a general dimensionless tree graph (
11
), an
N
-site
massive family tree
with the partial order
\mathscr{P}
is defined by the following integral:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{\mathscr{P}(1\cdots N)}\big\rrbracket\equiv\sum_{\mathsf{a}=\pm}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\Big[{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a})(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}z_{i}}\Big]\prod_{\alpha=1}^{I}R_{\mathsf{a}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}(r_{(\alpha i)}z_{i},r_{(\alpha j)}z_{j}),

(185)

where
z_{i}\equiv E_{i}\tau_{i}
is the dimensionless time variable of Site
i
,
r_{(\alpha i)}\equiv K_{\alpha}/E_{i}
,
R_{\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})
is the inhomogeneous and retarded bulk propagator for a scalar of mass
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha}
:

\displaystyle R_{\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})=\Big[\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\pm\mp}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})-\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mp\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})\Big]\theta(r_{j}z_{j}-r_{i}z_{i}),

(186)

and
\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0muD\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\mathsf{a}\mathsf{b}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}
is given in (
3
)-(
5
). Clearly, the retarded propagator
R_{\pm}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu)}(r_{i}z_{i},r_{j}z_{j})
is endowed with a time direction flowing from Site
i
to Site
j
. For convenience, we call
i
the mother of
j
and thus
j
the daughter of
i
. Then the partial order
\mathscr{P}(1\cdots N)
is defined with respect to this time flow, which, as explained in the main text, corresponds to the induced order of the graph, and the ancestor of the whole family tree is the maximal energy site.

We see that a massive family tree is fully specified by a partially ordered tree structure, together with a set of parameters
(E_{i},p_{i})

(i=1,\cdots,N)
, and
(K_{i},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i})

(i=2,\cdots,N)
, and the label of line variables
(K_{i},\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{i})
is identified with the label of the vertex to which the line is directed. With this understood, we only need to spell out the labels of all vertices when specifying a massive family tree. This observation motivates us to use a shorthand notation for massive family trees, similar to the notation for family trees in
[
68
]
. Here we quote the rule for this notation:

1.

Every massive family tree integral is represented by a string of numbers within a pair of blackboard-bold square brackets
\big\llbracket{\cdots}\big\rrbracket
. (We use
\big\llbracket{\cdots}\big\rrbracket
to distinguish the massive family tree with the original family tree notation
\big[\cdots\big]
introduced in
[
68
]
.)

2.

The leftmost number denotes the earliest member. If a mother has only one daughter, write the daughter’s number directly to the right of the mother’s number. If a mother has more than one daughter, use a pair of parentheses to enclose the
entire
subfamily of each of these daughters, and write all these subfamilies (in parentheses) to the right of the mother number. The order of subfamilies at the same level is arbitrary. Here, a subfamily of a site means herself and all her descendants.

Here are a few examples which illustrate how the rule works:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1234}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{a}=\pm}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Big[{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a})(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}z_{i}}\Big]R_{12}R_{23}R_{34},

(187)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{12(3)(4)}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{a}=\pm}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Big[{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a})(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}z_{i}}\Big]R_{12}R_{23}R_{24},

(188)

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{1(23)(4)}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{a}=\pm}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Big[{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a})(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}z_{i}}\Big]R_{12}R_{14}R_{23},

(189)

where we write
R_{\mathsf{a}}^{(\mkern 2.0mu\widetilde{\mkern-2.0mu\nu\mkern-2.0mu}\mkern 2.0mu_{\alpha})}(r_{(\alpha i)}z_{i},r_{(\alpha j)}z_{j})=R_{ij}
.

Although the notation above is designed to express a partially ordered tree structure, it is also straightforward to generalize it to an arbitrary directional tree without partial orders. As described in Sec.
5.1
, in a general directional tree graph, we can still introduce an induced partial order, with respect to which all lines are either “correct” or “wrong.” Then, we can simply add a back-pointing arrow to mark out all the wrong lines. For instance:

\displaystyle\big\llbracket{12\overleftarrow{3}4}\big\rrbracket=

\displaystyle\sum_{\mathsf{a}=\pm}\int_{-\infty}^{0}\prod_{i=1}^{4}\Big[{\mathrm{d}}z_{i}\,(\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a})(-z_{i})^{p_{i}}e^{\mathrm{i}\mathsf{a}z_{i}}\Big]R_{12}R_{32}R_{34}.

(190)

Clearly, once we allow for wrong lines in a family tree, the notation above is no longer unique. There could be many different ways to express the same directional tree graph, which is however not a problem for us.

Appendix B
Special Functions

All massive family trees and their augmentation/flattening are multivariate hypergeometric functions. However, only a handful of simple cases are named, and even fewer are well studied. In this appendix, we collect the definitions of a few named hypergeometric functions appearing in the main text.

Gamma functions

As a preparation, we introduce a shorthand notation for products of Euler
\Gamma
functions:

\displaystyle\Gamma[a_{1},\cdots,a_{n}]\equiv\Gamma(a_{1})\cdots\Gamma(a_{n}),

(191)

It is also convenient to abbreviate ratios of
\Gamma
products as:

\displaystyle\Gamma\begin{bmatrix}a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}\\
b_{1},\cdots,b_{n}\end{bmatrix}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma[a_{1},\cdots,a_{m}]}\,}{\Gamma[b_{1},\cdots,b_{n}]}.

(192)

We also draw heavy use of the Pochhammer symbol
(a)_{n}
, defined as:

\displaystyle(a)_{n}\equiv\displaystyle\frac{\,{\Gamma(a+n)}\,}{\Gamma(n)}.

(193)

Hypergeometric functions

The most well-known hypergeometric function is probably Gauss’s hypergeometric function, which is defined by the following series when it is convergent and by its analytical continuation outside the convergence region:

\displaystyle{}_{2}\text{F}_{1}\bigg[\begin{matrix}a,b\\
c\end{matrix}\bigg|z\bigg]\equiv\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\displaystyle\frac{\,{(a)_{n}(b)_{n}}\,}{(c)_{n}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{z^{n}}\,}{n!}.

(194)

In the main text, we use the dressed version of Gauss’s hypergeometric function, which is defined as

\displaystyle{}_{2}\mathcal{F}_{1}\bigg[\begin{matrix}a,b\\
c\end{matrix}\bigg|z\bigg]\equiv{\Gamma\bigg[\begin{matrix}a,b\\
c\end{matrix}\bigg]}{}_{2}\text{F}_{1}\bigg[\begin{matrix}a,b\\
c\end{matrix}\bigg|z\bigg]=\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}\Gamma\bigg[\begin{matrix}a+n,b+n\\
c+n\end{matrix}\bigg]\displaystyle\frac{\,{z^{n}}\,}{n!}.

(195)

The most studied bivariate hypergeometric functions are Appell series
[
94
]
. In the paper, we use the (dressed) Appell
\text{F}_{4}
function, which is defined by the following series and its analytical continuation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{4}\bigg[\begin{matrix}a,b\\
c_{1},c_{2}\end{matrix}\bigg|x,y\bigg]\equiv\sum_{m,n=0}^{\infty}\Gamma\bigg[\begin{matrix}a+m+n,b+m+n\\
c_{1}+m,c_{2}+n\end{matrix}\bigg]\displaystyle\frac{\,{x^{m}y^{n}}\,}{m!n!}.

(196)

At the level of
n
-variate hypergeometric functions, known results are rather sparse. Among them a relatively well-studied class is Lauricella’s functions
[
91
]
. In this paper, we used the Lauricella’s
\text{F}_{\text{C}}
function, defined as:

\displaystyle\text{F}_{\text{C}}\left[\begin{matrix}a,b\\
c_{1},\cdots,c_{N}\end{matrix}\middle|z_{1},\cdots,z_{N}\right]\equiv\sum_{n_{1},\cdots,n_{N}=0}^{\infty}(a)_{n_{1\cdots N}}(b)_{n_{1\cdots N}}\prod_{i=1}^{N}\displaystyle\frac{\,{1}\,}{(c_{i})_{n_{i}}}\displaystyle\frac{\,{z_{i}^{n_{i}}}\,}{n_{i}!}.

(197)
